is concerned. With a coach-built sidecar par- ticular care has to be taken in the cleaning, however. If you try to rub off the dirt when it is dry you will inevitably scratch -and, line the paint and quite ruin it. Even dust should not be rubbed off dry, as it is gritty stuff and will score a number of 'fine lines., Both dust and mud should be thoroughly soaked off, then swished with a sopping sponge. After sponging, go over the paint with a leather wrung out in clean water until Practically dry. A dab of car polish, applied with a soft rag and finished off with a clean, dry leather will give a fine' shine. A spot of paraffin in the first bucket of water will remove nattd that has been allowed to dry hard on .the coach work, but plenty of fresh water will be needed afterwards to remove the resulting Smears. The only thing to remove tar stains is a spot of fresh butter (not salt) and plenty of elbow grease, applied immediately you get home.

CYCLING AB MOTORING.

TRUING SLIGHTLY OVAL WHEELS.

After a slight accident, it will frequently be found that, while the wheel is true sideways, it will have become slightly oval, or egg-shaped. If it is badly 'out of truth in this direction, of course, it will require rebuilding, but if only slight correction is necessary it is,a very simple matter to put things to rights. The first thing it to find the spots at which the wheel bulges. The simplest way to do this is to remove the tyre, replace the wheel in the forks, strejch a piece .of wire ,across the' forks and fix it just above, and as close as possible to, the rim, and spin the wheel. The rim will touch the .wire at the spots where it bulges, and these spots should be marked with_ chalk. Again turn the wheel slowly, ascertain the low spots in the rim, and mark ;these with, a different sort- of chalk mark. Having found out all the pieces at which the wheel is untrue, take your spoke adjuster first loosen the spokes at the low places and then tighten up those nearest the bulges. Test the rim' frequently during the adjustments or probably you will make matters worse than they- were originally. For the same reason, only screw up or unscrew each spoke a half-turn at a time. Your aim must be to have all the spokes-'of the rim at the same tension, and this you can gauge approximately by feeling them with your fingers. Don't forget to file down the ends of any screwed-up spokes that are projecting beyond the rim.

HOW TO CARRY YOUR CAPE. Even on a brightsummer's day it nnsafe

• to start out on anything approaching a long -Aide withont a waterpnoof of some sort or 'another, so ficklei.A..onio. eliula*,tA is the best form Of protection. Nit This note is not to discuss which is the best covering, hut rather to point out that the active service life of the average 'waterproof is cut down to half what it ought to be by the method usually adopted of .squeezing it into a tight sausage-like roll and strapping it beneath the saddle, where • it catches much of the mud that sprays up from , the wheel. ,The most careful cyclist I know • wraps his cape—a three-year-old by the way, and still going strong—in 'oil skin and secures it by, means of two straps to the handlebar. Tightly rolling the "cape does more to destroy the weather resisting qualities of a waterproof than anything else.

SOOTEED SPARKING PLUGS.

Many motor cycles, especially two-stroke engines are seriously affected in their' running powers owing to the fact that the sparking Plugs have become sooted. So many of the two-stroke engines are. both oil and air cooled, and thus it is not surprising that a certain amount of plug trouble should be experietnced. It is quite an easy patter successfully to overcome the diffi- culty. As. a general -rule' the plug, is at right angles to .e 4 cylinder; and in this case a single point plug should be obtained and washers fixed between the plug and the cylinder in such a manner that the Point is upright. This causes the oil -to drain away from the point, thus eliminating in a large measure the sooting of the plug. When the magneto allows it, a plug should be used with a large 'gap, as this makes it practically impossible for the oil to bridge the points. It is a-Nery good plan oceasionally to remove the plug and soak it in petrol all .nlght-: This removes any burnt caoon that there may be.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
TO CLEAN YOUR CAR.

Many motorists are nowadays looking after their own cars, their chauffeurs having either enlisted or become engaged on .munitions. In the pre-war days—how long ago they seem !—the fact that the car was always bright and clean seemed only natural,- and few, people dreamt how much labour had been expended to have it alwakys spick and span. • Personally I have never possessed a .chaiiffeur, and so the new order of things has hardly affected me. But there are .a few hints that, if followed, will greatly facilitate the maintenance of the smart appearance .of your car. 'grease marks from dirty lingers are a common form of disfigure- ment. Ordinary washing will not remove..thern, and, of course, the varnish suffers consider#y if either paraffin or benzoic!, is used. A good soap lather is the best thing to use. Afterwards swilling from the hose will comtdete the work. The hood can be cleaned by "scrubbing with soap and water, but it should be left up until dry. When the cushions become wet, they should be dried slowly: in moderate heat. Linseed oil is a capital agent to employ for taking off spots left by the mud drying on the body work. ; Waddidg is a good material for applying the oil. The same liquid will remove rain stains off the bonnet. If the woodwork is occasionally polished with some of the special preparations sold for the purpose it adds to the appearance of the car.

On Saturday afternoon the funeral took place at the Daresbury Churchyard of the late Mr. Stephen, Plumpton, /managing director of' Messrs. George Plumpton, Ltd., Albion Works, Warrington. The funeral was largely attended. The Vicar of Daresbury officiated. The principal mourners were: 3lrs: Plumpton (widow), Messrs. Herbert, ,George and Frank Plumpton (brothers),. Tom 'and Sam Plumpton (nephews), W. Taylor (brother-in-law), Birken- head;- Mrs. F. Plumpton (Sister-in-law), Mr. E. A. Bradbury (cousin), Mrs. Taylor (mother-in- law), Birkenhead; - and Messrs. H. Cosgrove, C. S. Burgess, W. Parsons and John Moss. Others presenf were Messrs. C. ,Gough, George Wal- lington and,' T. F. Wallington. The firm was represented by Mr. George Plumpton (chairman of the company). The hymns sung were "For ever with the Lord" and "On . the resurrection morning," both favourites of Mr. Plumpton. . Mr. John Wright was at the organ, and pri9r to the com- mencement of the service he played "0 rest in, the Lord," and at the conclusion the Dead March in "Saul."

THE ROYAL ACADEMY.

"The Royal Academy Illustrated" (Walter Judd, Ltd., price 25.) isgued by authority, con- tains over 200 admirable reproductions,in photo- gravure of the pictures and sculptar.n in this year's exhibition, which will be notable for many fine works that the war has inspired. The reproductions include the work of 146 R.A.'s and A.R.A.'s. The book is the first hook of its kind issued by authority of the Royal Academy. It gives one a general conception of the spirit underlying the best work in this year's exhilli- tion, and forms a most interesting souvenir for all who desire a permanent record of the prin- cipal exhibits and for all those who cannot visit the Academy. Local interest in the book is based upon the picture of' a bronze bust of the late John Cros. field. by Henry Pegram, that was recently placed in the Warrington Town Hall by the members of his family. There are several of the works of Sir Luke Fildes, and portraits of Lord Derby ,ad Sir W. H. Lever. s

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MAY 27, 1916.

JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

Barber-ous.

The barber loomed large in last week's issue, .and I. have somewhat to write respecting him. The very employment of, the common term ha.,,1 caused searching of heart. However it may be with the younger generation in this indispen- sable trade, members of the old school were apt to resent it. I knew one at Elland, in the West Riding (he had gone there from Wharfedale a‘ a young man) in the far4Af days, when that 'district was included in pay itinetary, who, over- hearing the word " barber " in the waiting customers' conversation, would draw .himself up in offended" dignity and interposO the protest., " ' Barber,' sir! 'Hairdresser,' please. My apprenticeship indentures distinctly stated that I was bound to "learn the business of a- hair- dresser; the word ' barber ' never occurred." But perhaps my Yorkshire acquaintance was an exception—like that of the learAdVossius, whose barber, according to Steele, "used to comb his head in iambics." Yes, I think he must have been exceptional, for he ended by cutting his throat with one of his own razors, whicii is. as rare as fora sailor to commit suicide by drown- ing or a chemist by poison. Modern Innovation.

can well imagine how scandalised I hat poor old fellow would have been could he had foreseen the advent of the lady barber-4 mean hair- dresser,- with apologies to his shade. I note that at last week's local Tribunal an insurance agent obtained temporary exemption on the Plea that he iyas instructing his wife in the business. I have seen such lady agents already at work. • I have just' come• across a remarkable case more to my present point. The other • clay I accepted a friend's invitation to a motor run.- It was a 'hurried call, and taking train to what used to be known.aS "th' w.eyver's seeahpport," I joined hitn at-Littleborough, whence we had a joy ride over Blackstoncdge. We -deseended by way of 'Cragg Vale, and at 'llytholmroyd I called a halt for ,a 'shave. ' The operation was deftly per- formed by the tradesman's wife, and I learned that he, too, had gained a short respite from military service in order to teach her to use 'razor and scissors, and so keep the business going I until his return. She had proved a smart pupil, and when I called 'she was in sole 'charge. Of course, lady, hairdressers have been known for years in some of the great cities. but this is a noteworthy bit of war service. More power to the brave woman's elbow. Plainly, she deserv.ts the encouragement and active support of the • A Bad Spoc. _ _

I knew one in that same trade who made an unprofitable change just before the war. He ana three or four friends arranged to embark on a hut. building sveculaiion in Canada, and he was so confident of immediate .and permanent success that in winding up his affairs he often made the mistake, in the hearing of customers, cf counting his chickens before thy were hatched. In a word, the scheme turned out a disastrous failure, and the once so boastful and now chastened barber is already back in this country and engaged in his former occupation, though not in the same lOcality.

Business Improving. " Vigilant's" bit of "Gossip" to the effect that the hot weather was " dtEmping men into hairdressers' shops" and that barbers confess that such weather conditions practically double their business (which must-be particularly wel- come now so many men are away), reminded me of a "Punch" tit-bit which appeared in a similar.li'eek to. last :quite a genetation.-ago. "How'd you like it, sir?" asked the hairdreiiser, as he flourished the scissors above the head of the exhausted customer. "Phew !" was the response of the desperate one, "cut it to the bone."

===== 0002601_19160527_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
Nicknames.

Coroner Brighouse hears with Professed in- difference of some calling him "Old Sam," and indeed he is in good company. A pliblic man may wIP feal,flattcred when‘suclt familiar per- sonal refereneerbeCOnie common--as, for ex- ample, "Bobby" Lowe and "Jimmy" Low- ther. After all, to be strict, these are not nick- names; though by no mean,s so nice, that of "Soapy. Sam" for the' famdus Bishop Wilber= force, approximates more nearly thereto. I have come across more amusing instances, but .I fear-I have 'previously given at least some of these, and, burdened with a treacherous memory, one' naturally desires to avoid repeti- tion as far as possible. - r Empire Day. _ _

• AS I contemplated Wednesday's celebratiOn, my mind went back to a letter ,addressed to the Press in view of a previous festival of the kind. ,It was from the hon. secretary of the School Peace League, and was. signed "Carl Heath." Teachers were thereby invited to °"impress upon the children the humaner and more civilised aspects o British rule as an organised advance in pacification rather than as development of mere force and power." "Empire Day," pur- sued the writer in elaboration of his argument, "as a glorification of an Empire separated in its sympathies from and antagonistic to the rest of the world; Empire Day as a: flag-waving sen- timentalism,- has no iust place in the schools of a civilised nation. Empire Day as a day for the consideration of the duties to humanity of a community of peoples having splendid oppor- tunities for the Oromotion of human liberty, peace and justicevis a matter that may be vital to us all." Ah, well, well ! " Little Willie."

The "Stage and, Kinema " item concerning Miss Lilian Maitland's debut as "Little Willie" in "East Lynne," sent me afresh to my num- ber of the current "Printers' Pie." Many readers of this column promptly obtained copies, and, with deference to • the superior judgment of more competent critics, I venture to submit that, if not just as original as some, one of the best jokes therein is "The doctor's dileMma." The contributor, H. M. Brock, shows in the surgery a Scottish laddie, having by the hand a younger one in tears, and accompanied by a little sister. Says, the sthall boy: "If ye please, doctor, we've coora to pay the bill. Here's half-a-crown in siller, twa shillings in copper, eighteenpence in stamps; and saxpence in Wullie, and will ye get it oot the noo, because we're late for school ?" The rendering of the, dialect is not impeccable, ye ken, but as a bit of "child study" the sketch is—well, simply immense, to use a convenient modern expression. Snobbery.

The performers mentioned last week as appear- ing at the " Hip " here and nightly returning. to Manchester for bed and board, set me think- ing of others I have met, actresses and singers especially, whg have treated after that manner hospitable towns whose inhabitants flocked to patronise the entertainment, and . who felt rather snubbed when they discovered that their leading hotel, on which they were wont to pride themselves, was beneath the notice of the dainty creature who, had condescended to give them a. series of turns extending over a week. Once in those parts we had actually a County Court ' Judge made of that sort of stuff. His home was in London. and when he was Ai circuit nothing inferior to Manchester would serve—and his Honour had the extremely bad taste to tell us so in a tactless speech, from the Bench. Warrington may not be good enough for such people as a nermanent abode, bytt. in the absence tof any explanation, one might have sunnoseA they Could have endured a first-class local hotel for a week's engagement.

Military Etiquette. Should a wounded soldier sporting a panama raise it or salUte on meeting a. lady acquaint- ance? The point is raised by " Critic "in Lymm "Facts and Fancies." Even assuming it to be put as a serious proposition, and that my brother scribe will. not laugh at me for my pains i my own experience were hardly likely to help to a wlution. I have known a soldier re- fuse to reffiove his hat in a Civil Court, and stoutly defend his attitude when challenged; on the other hand. I have known one, on being shown into the editorial sanctum, uncover at once and show the utmost deference to the pen which is said to be mightier than the sword. On the whole, I incline to the opinion that. sup- posing him to be native and not. Colonial, the wearer of the non-military headgear might so far condescend as to " tip " the panama "just a wee."

A great deal of talent is lost to the world for the want of a little courage.—Sydney Smith

£1 FOR 15s. 6D.

The Working Men's Chance To Become Successful Investors.

\\R SAVINGS CERTIFICATE.

The proposal to institute War Savings Associations in Warrington is one which is deserving of support from all truly patriotic individuals, not only from the standpoint of the immediate national need, but because of the salutary effect that it promises tO have upon people who as yet have never learned the real meaning of saving.. .

Speaking generally, the opportunity now pre- sents itself to the workers to rally to the support of the Exchequer ,in providing the money s.) very necessary for carrying on this great war and ensuring our ability to,stay the course to the end. The people whose good fortune it is to be in receipt of over £3OO per annum would gladly invest £5OO in this exceedingly reintinerative Government security if they were allowed to do so. Those unfortunate People in receipt of a fixed wage or -salary who have tht.), foresight to appreciate the necessity for saving and, who would gladly take advantage, of the present offer, are, alas, in most cases, so heavily hit by the increased cost of living and the multitude of calls upon their Very diminished sovereign, that they are unable to invest to any great ex- tent in War Savings Certificates. " Take for instance a municipal officer, say a clerk or a married assistant master in an elementary school. AS things are at the present moment, through no fault of his .employers, the wage is hardly to be regarded . as more than a ',lying wage. In fact, ir almost every case reliance has to be placed on ' some other source of income or on past savings in order to meet the calls upon him, and to keep up the appearance which the very natur, ' of his employment and social standing demands. The likelihood of any extensive investment among this class of people is very small. Where then are the people to' whom our local com- mittee must look for the succesSful miming of the associations?

===== 0002601_19160527_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
WORK AND MONEY PLENTIFUL.

The people in our large. works are the fruitful field, among whom we must hope to gather.ihe harvest of war savings. Agreed 'immediately, that all engineers are not earning £5 a week, and that many are not in receipt of a much better wage, as far as purchasing power. goes, than they were in pre-war days, still there are large number—a very considerable nuniber— of families in the town where the income has been very materially increased. 'Evidences of this are innumerable. There are woinen folk who have gone out to work who. previously stayed at home; boys and. girls o'n work that_is very highly paid—too highly 'paid,, unless there is the good sense accompanying the earning lo set aside some 6f the " extra " for a possible "rainy day." Work was never better nor money more plentiful among the working classes, than it is to-day, despite the very high cost of living. But this has not called for that farsighted bolicy of saving, that one would faia see among these people. The fact is , the majority of people in every "class," rich, inter- mediath 'and poor, have no idea of the value of saving.

With -working class folk this is perhaps due to a lack of opportunity; more probably to the want of an object lesson to bring it home te them. Often if a man save it is not for a remote contirlgency, such as old age, sickness or, uneinPloyment. These are allowed to take care of themselves. There must' be a definite object in view; a cup final, a walking day out- ing, or, in a few cases, a "house of furniture." Some stimulus muSt be there to .proauce the desire to save. And this is not real saying after all, but a holding back of money for a short time to spend it in a lump sum, frequently for no lasting benefit. The number of working class people who "set up housekeeping" without a halfpenny to fall back on is really astour4. rk,: and to the handi- cap of a hire-pnrchase debt are added the in- creasing responsibilities which these people so easily call down upon themselves. "It is good for a man that he bear the yoke in his youth" is a true saying as far as saving is concerned. It is then to the young people in our w‘orks that attention should be given by the local Central War Savings Committee at the present time. The sixpences aid shillings so easily coaxed from them by the football match, the theatre and the cinema can be diverted into channels far more useful and much more valuable. Apart from the national gain as far as the war is concerned, the future result will be even more far-reaching and beneficial. 'The saving habit once formed is not easily lost, and now is the time to cultivate it.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
REPORTING PROGRESS.

Too much emphasis, however, Cannot be laid upon the need to'keep the investors alive as to their financial position as the months go by. It is not enough to assure them that the money is "safe," and that interest will be added each year and (after the first 12: months) each month: .The local Central Committee or the secretary should prepare a list each quarter for a general meeting of associates, giving clearly the position of each member. The fact that it tells them on the back of a 'War Savings Book how they stand is not sufficknt. 'This' demands too much " faith " for people not used to investment. Their savings must have a living reality for them. Perhaps this attitude of Mind is strangq to many, but it is a very important factor in their minds. In fact do we not find it among every section? The ,morning meal is very often enjoyed more because of a rise of 3d. or 6d. in the particular shares held,. not as a speculation, but as a permanent investment by the business man. He likes to see how. things are, although he has no thought of selling out.. To part 'with money and never to hear of it for five years is too much for the average .-rorking man. , . Special- emphasis also must be laid upon the fact that the certificates can be cashed im- mediately for their face value if a time of neces- sity demands it. By applying to the association secretary the 15s: 6d. is returnable, and the /certificate then becomes the property of the association. I.ctily people are content to receive a very small rate of interest or even ,to forfeit interest altogether if the principal is " get-at- able " easily: Of course, one of the great educativt sides of. these associations will, be to encourage the real saving habit.'

===== 0002601_19160527_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
OPPORTUNITY TO EDUCATE;

A tactful secretary can soon achieve this very necessary piece of social work and thus prevent the calamitous poverty which - reveals itself.. among the wage-earners during periods of trade depression or labour 'disputes. IV is not many years • since the :pitiable spectacle was seen in• Warrington of applicants for free meals at .soup kitchans being fed by ellow-Workmen who had been in receipt of less wages Apart' froth the ' injustice to the careful an more, intelligent man it was a reflection upon the people who ate readiest to cry out for a living wage. The opportunity to educate these. well-meaning . but thoughtless citizens, now. presents itself in the: form of a very. necessary and patriotic scheme for raising a people's war loan. For the sake of the future of' our nation -t. is to be 'hoped' that suceegs -will attend the efforts of the War- rington Central War Savings Committee. S. J.

CONSCIENCES.

The Bishop Of Liverpool On The Duties • Of Citizenship.

The Bishop of Liverpool having been asked by a conscientious objector to military service what is his "attitude toward such objectors, and if he will make it public, has sent the follow- ing reply:— The Palace, Liverpool, May 22. 1916.

, Dear Mr. . ,—I have - very decided vpinions indeed about conscientious objectors fo military service. I think that if they decline to fight for their country and are content to let, other people do •it for them, their duty is to leave their own shores and to ta,ke up their resi- dence in some other land where they will not be called upon to bear arms. Freedom of con- science does not mean believing anything that you like, even though it be to the detriment of your neighbours, and declining to perform your duties as Christian men and citizens. This is certainly not the freedom of conscience for which our forefathers kontended, and for Vhich, if alive, they w9121d hae contended to-day. ,

WORKERS' PATRIOTISM.

Mr. J. A. Seddon Says Labour must Be Rewarded.

NATION,LISE RAILWAYS AND SHIPPING.

Having agreed that England is worth fighting for, as, indeed, all right-thinking people have (as "The Spectator" would say), supporters of ,the National Workers' League held a meet- ing in the Co-operative Hall on Sunday evening to advocate schemes whereby after the war England shall also be more of a place' worth living in for the workers. Their particular desires are :—The national ownership and control of vital industries; the establishment on a democratic basis of defences adequate to the Empire's security, the federation of BritAh Free States, the modification of our international commercial policy, educational reform, agricultural reform, and' adult suffrage. Mr. Stephen °Walsh, M.P. for l.nce, and Councillor Sexton, of Liverpool, had been advertised among the list of sp-...akers, but they were unable to be present. Mr. Jr Elwell (the chairman) .and Mr. J. A. Seddon, -,therefore, held the attention of the audience. Mr. Seddon, it will be remembered with pride, has been 'a Government missionary in France land the United States, explaining to the people of these. countries our view,point. in' the war, and what we are doing to,gainsuccess. When in Los Angeles, in Decenlber,-.1914, he recalled, he met a gentleman wearing clerical garb, who had been ministering to English-speak- ing Catholics in Berlin at the time war was declared. His position gave him access to many influential quarters, and on the 3rd August, he said, he was sitting in the crack cavalry club hi Berlin with two high officers, one of whom ceased . conversing .to go to the tape machine. Coming back to. them he said,, "England has joined with France and Russia. We are beaten, 'I but we will bankrupt Europe, and when. that has been consummated we will all, start off the same mark, and the real Victor of the war will be tlie nation with the greatest recuperative power, and that will be Germany,. because she is the most scientific country in the world." 'Germany, comthented Mr. Seddon with in- tense seriousness, is fighting a" lost cause in the hope that When Europe has been bled white she 'will recover most quickly and then beat the world, but we are not going to let her do- it. War is no rose-water, but nations have decreed that certain devilish. instruments ‘. shall be tabooed.' and, yet, in defiance- of all this, Ger- many has broken, the rules. I have always tried. to avoid a feeling of revenge, but when I saw in France MY own countiymen broken in body and many of them with their minds gone because of being helpless victims of poison gas and, liouid flame. I asked myself, Ts there no nenaltv against them for this? (Applause.) Her fighting at sea hi,s been cheracterised by the same outrage of the prin- ciples of naval warfare, whereas we have always fought fairly and -humanely. No one can say of our men that they have left.men to drown if they 'could ,be saved. (Applause.)

Confidence In Jack.

I won't forget, in a hurry crossing the Atlantic after the' war had beEn in progress about six; months, and the whole- of the German raiders had not been tied' up. As we got beyond the three-mile limit outside New York two 'British sentinels came to see that there were no enemies on beard and,, having ;left us, we then resumed: our 3,000-nile run. All the way there was abiding confidence in the British Navy, and when I. asked an American lady how it was that she had such-great' faith:•inx the Navy she, replied, "Well, you know, ybu have got, a tradition. However brutal soldiers may be, the British Navy have ,alays proved them- selves to be gentlemen of the first water." (Applause.) This was not an ordinary fight of nations' against nations, the speaker continued. It was one of ideals against ideals. Let Germany pre- dominate and our institution y would Melt away -like snow befere _the storm. We, must 'put up with no inconclusive peace, for' that would mean that the inenaCe of Another war would. hang over us. At present we were probably at tin most i_.ritical stage in the struggle', and in the next six months the fate of Europe during the next hundred years would be decided. Some- one had said that the differences between the Allies and the enemy were not 'worth the sacri- fice of one human life. 'That person must be dead to the .tremendous issues involved. (Hear, hear.) "We of the British Workers' League, say that this war is a righteous war," deelared Mr. Seddon, adding .that after the war work must be found for everyone. There must be no unemployment, and everyone must have a living wage. • '

They could not allow a monopoly to strangle trade, ad pay starVation wages. , NEW BOOKS.

\ "The European Anarchy." . By E. Lows Diel4nson. --- London: George Allen and Tinwin, Ltd. 28. 6d: net.._ _ _ , •

I I Mr, Lewes Dickinson has Written a number of pamphlets dealing' with the present inter- national criSis since the ,war broke out, and his I point of view is fairly well-known. In theiPfie- , sent book he gives a short account of the policies .of the diifferent belligerent countries with the object of showing that European anarchy is the cause,of the war, lie does not dispute the I fact that 'Germany pretipitated the outbreak: he frankly recognises this, but regards it as only the superficial cause, the real cause lying far deeper, among the conflicting ambitions and .mutual suspicions of the different European nations. . He devotes considerable space to an, examination of the policy of Germany, in which the shows admirable impartiality and fairness. lie rejects the theory that the war is the result of a predetermined policy on the part of Ger- many as improbable. The section of , his book in which Mr. Lowes Dickinson discusses "Opinion in Germany " is extremely interesting and valuable, as is also the section dealing with "Opinion about Etermany." • Mr. LOWeS 'Dickinson supports the views he puts forward by cogent argument and quota- tion. He makes great use of the opinions ex- pressed at various dates anterior to the war by various Belgian diplomats, and published since the war comAienced, as to the direction in which events were moving during the last few years! Whether the time is yet ripe for the solution of Present troubles which he puts forward we may at least be permitted to question. But that there is a not inconsiderable amcunt of truth in much that he urges, and that it is desirable that our people should recognise thiS, we do not for one moment doubt: We can very cordially recommend the book as being one of the most dispassionate and judicial works published on the war.

FARM AND GARDEN.

THE MILK SUPPLY.

Mr. R. H, Green, of Willesborough, in a letter to the " Times " points out that the dis- tributhig companies in London, as a rule, insist upon ,their contracts beginning and ending in spring, when everybody's cow has a ,calf. when cost of production is reduced,. and when milk is plentiful. The result is that once a farmer has been unwise enough to enter into this arrangement he is for ever in the hands of the middlemen. He suggests that farmers should make their contracts to begin and end at Michaelmas. Here lies excellent ground f(•r combination on :the part of farmers to throttic. a growing evil completely.

PIGS FOR PROFIT.

The. old, old question, "Do pigs pay ?" is not in fashion just now, when prime small bacon' pigs are fetching nearly is. per lb., says "Farm and Home." The, actual average price is 12s. 9d. per stone, and in at least one market (Derby) the full _shilling has been recorded. At present values there is no doubt at all about the profit,. notwithstanding the bigh cost of food, but it would obviously be foolish to found. an., argument in favour of bacon production generally, on •results at • such a time as. this.. Recent events have brought the pig into •great prominence, for in Germany the large stock always -kept in time of peace has proved an enormous advantage, while we in England are suffering for our. past neglect, :but all that,,,is beside -the question as to whether a good , fair prOfit can always' be made Thy .skilful -manage- ment. I think the answer is decidedly " But it is not a business that can ,be successfully. conducted by haphazard treatment. Public trials • have recently shown that t acon can at the present 'moment be produced at gd. per lb. up to a certain age and weight, but that beyond that limit the cost rapidly increases. That is a most important point to bear in mind, and an unanswerable argument in 'favour %bf early maturity and marketing. Th.en 'there is the question of breed and that of the selection of foods.. in both of which may be founl a mar-, gin of difference between profit and. loss. Prob- ably the food is the, more potent factor of the two. To be at all sparing with the rations is a fatal mistake.' The pig should always have as much as he can clear up-, and, as regards quality the best is the cheapest. A. combination which cannot be beaten is one of barley meal, potatoes, tlnd. 'skim milk, but the potatoes should be, steamed and mot boiled.

THE SUMMER PASTURES.

We, may take it ler granted that all . classes of stock are no 7 at grass or mainly dePendent upon green food, though: with heavy rainfalls and a low temperature it can scarcely be said the month. has been as favourable ag one could wiSh, •

-Wherever sourness of pasture prevails—a state, of affairs easily discernible by noticing the rank appearance of the grass—it is best to run the scythe over such patches, and us the cdarse fodder thus obtained for toiving haystacks, and so. forth. By adopting this course there is a possibility of making the land really productive, instead of being a source of loss. Another way to increaSe our yield of stock food from on-v; summer pastures is by scattering the dropping as frequently as possible. When they are allowed to remain untouched it means the loss of almost a square yard of grass fer the reinain- der of -the. summeri for it will net be until the autumn, 'when the early frosts have sweetened the rank herbage, that the grass Obtained from this overdose of manure will become .accept- able. On the other band, if, . when • tb., pasture has been closely fed, it is the rule to knock the manure ,about, the .;ilighter dressing thereby obtained will''leave no unpleasant effects; the sward will remain as even :is a lawn, and there will be an absence of the rank, tufts which so often disfigure an ,uneveilly-gravd pasture. IN THE GARDEN.

EAR_LY POTATOES.

'As we are not yet out of the v.-ood as" requrds frost it will be advisable where the .hrtalm of potatoes is just peering through the soil to give this some protection .by drawing \a \littlesil over it, and ,thus it maybe save the er.2p. We have also with dd,S.antdge used i little loose litter, evergreen boughs, or hc4s coverediwith canvas. '

THINNiNG THE BLOOMS ON FRUIT TREES.

A friend of mine who, last Autumn, had planted a number of fruit trees, including apples, pears, plums, &c., was telling me how delighted he was 'on seeing so many flower buds on. the trees. Far too many owners of fruit trees think they have made a. very !bad' bargain' if the trees they have just nlanted do not bear fruit the first season after planting. • I told my friend, that if he desired the trees to do well in coming years he ought to remove all the flower bads,- except in cases where. he was anxious to verify the name of any particular variety—evltn then only leaving sufficient- for the' purpose. The great point as regards newly-planted trees is to encourage. them to make free growth the first year, and this is impossible if the trees ore allowed to 'carry a crop. By removing the Rower buds we conAentrate the whole energy of the tree into the g‘rowth of branches and roots. The more freely a tree grows in its younger stages the greater arc the prospects of realising a full crop in years to come.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
STAKING PEAS.

Early staking no doubt helps the young plants in many ways, as it protects them, and also checks the attack of sparrows and wood pigeons,, Which, in wooded districts;,' do great damage to the young .plants if left unstaked long after they are through the ground. Before staking it is a good' plan to 'draw up a little ridge of., soil along' each side of the row—not close to the row—as this will help to retain any rain- that may . fall or, any-, artificially-applied water. All Too often we see the stick's so placed that the tops meet, 'with the inevitable result that by the time the plants reach their full height they become a tangled .masS. If the sticks- are set wider apart at the top than at the bottom the plants have room to branch out, thus increasing their bearing. All pea sticks should have 'abundance of spray on them, and be long ehough to reach one foot, or even two feet, above, the advertised, height of the variety. When the principal stakes have been placed in' position finish off by sticking between them short ,pieces of the spray cut off when trimming the supports, these giving the young plants a start until they reach the brushwood higher up. —Wick,- in "Farm and Home."

POULTRY FOR PROFIT.

THIS WEEK'S WORK IN BRIEF.

Shade is necessary for the growing stock dur- ing the hot weather. By placing the houses near a hedge or ditch the birds will take advantage of the natural shade.

A constant supply of clean water is necessarl. It should, always be kept in the shade. • Leave the doors of the houses wide open during the day; and the brooder top open from 10 aan• to 4 p.m.

Broody hens will be more plentiful now They should be placed in the coop, immediately they show signs of broodiness. Broodies are in great demand by bonmekecpers, and old birds should be disposed of in this way. The price obtained is satisfactory. Useful Meals far Summer Feeding.

The mash must be bulky. Malt culms and clover meals are light and bulky. Bran and biscuit meal are very useful for suminer feeding. Through the summer months, rich foods such as peameal and maizemeal mar be left out •of the mash, and the amount of green food in- creased,

Remember that during the hot weather mash will turn sour in a few hours. Should sour food Joe gi+en to chickens the results will be disastrous. It is best to prepare fresh mash for eachsmeal. Milk must be used very carefully for growing chickens. Flaked foods are very useful, since they are bulky and easily prepared.—" PoultrY World."

===== 0002601_19160527_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
BiTS FOR SEGINNERS.

The Use of Nest Eggs. The use of nest eggs ;will not increase the productiveness of the hens. They simply teach the hens to lay in a place chosen by the poultrr keeper, i\Test eggs, when filled with insect, poi-der, may be useful to keep .the vermin down, but ,the beginner must not use this method of keeping the setting' hen free from insects. • The Cause of Abnormal Eggs.. , The majority of abnormal, eggs are caused bi an over-fat condition, or are the result of en pver-stjmu.lating diet. The two chief causes a shell-less eggs are a shortage .of grit, and fright Pale-yolked 'eggs are usually the result of 3 shortage of green food or underfeeding.

The Indian Runner Ducks. The padian Runner duck is undoubtedly the most prolific layer of any duck we have, and if; will ,conipare..tvery. favourably With the average fowl. Cottagers or others with a plot of .land at their dipposal Might do worse than invest in 0, pen of Runners. , The Two Classes of Ducks. ' Ducks may. he divided into two classes—the egg-producer and- the 'table bird. In the first class maY be placed the Indian Runner, the Buff Orpington, the Khaki Campbell, the Coaley Fawn. In the second class may be placed the Aylesbury and the Rouen. A cross between. these ' two ,is .often advantageous. Although 'ducks may be successfully reared without swimming water, the results will be more satisfactory if this can be provided. At any rate, to rear ducklings on a large scale without swimming water would prove but un- satisfoctory and unprofitable .to the beginner.

Disposing of the Cockerels.

The cockerels from the February-hatched. broods- will be almost ready for the market. \ They. should be separated from the rest. of the I stock and fed on fattening foods such as Sussex ground oats, boiled maize, and barley or pes" The 'birds should be fed three time 13; day, .and their food should consist entirely 751 soft food. Do not all,ow the birds too much exercise, and see that the birds are not over- fed, otherwise loss of appetite will result. Give them as much -as they will clear up in ten k] minutes, and do not leave food in the troughs.-- " Poultry World."

===== 0002601_19160527_art0048.txt | included=true | included =====
REV. T. VICKERS.

Valedictory Presentations At

St. Clement's Mission.

A large and regretful audience assembled in St. Clement's Mission Room on Monday even- ing to pay an an revoir tribute to their worthy curate, the Rev. T. Vickers, who is leaving the parish to take up a post at Liverpool. Opportunity was .taken of making a presenta- tion of a purse of gold and a travelling bag, and as numerous compliments were paid to Mr. Vickers, and as he replied, tears fell from the eyes of several in the audience.

H. Houghton, in asking the Rector to make the presentation, said the example which Mr. Vickers had shown them was superb. His loving 'kindness to all was indescribable. Although his work there had finished, yet he was only passing from one sphere to another, and he hoped that it would be one of still greater usefulness. They all knew what he had done for them, and he was sure it was a very kind Providence that brought him in their track. On behalf of the congregation. of St. Clement's he offered him their most hearty thanks, and wished him God-speed. (ApPlauge.) In making the presentation, the Rector said it was with much feeling they met there that evening. They all regretted losing Mr. Vickers, and he was sure they were all grateful for what he had done for them during the past six and a half years. They had had some very hard- working curates, but he was sure none had done more than Mr. Vickers. He had led them on gently and wisely, and during all that time he (the Rector) had not heard a murmur against him. He, along with Mr. Buckmaster, might have left them two or three years ago to have gone to Padgate, but they preferred to work in town. Now the time had come that he should go, and he (the Rector) thought he was acting quite rightly in doing so. It was a great loss to him (the Rector) and also to the people. (Applause.) _ _

===== 0002601_19160527_art0049.txt | included=true | included =====
Loth to Go.

On rising to reply, the Rev. T. Vickers was greeted with loud and prolonged applause, the audience giving three hearty cheers and also singing " For he's a jolly good fellow." When thedeMonstration had subdued, Mr. Vickers said he had been glad to labour as a working parson among working people. He was grateful for all the support given to him, and would like to offer his sincere thanks to Miss Boardman, who, from the time he came to St. Clement's, .had done for him everything possible in her power. Mr. Houghton also had done everything pos- bible for five long years, and in him he always had a loyal friend. Mr. J. Whalley (caretaker) and his wife had rendered very good services, as also had the choir, sidesmen, Sfinday school teachers, Bible-class and sewing meeting. He was also very grateful to Capt. Sankey, Mr. Gibson and Mr. Essenhigh, of the C.L.8., and to many others too numerous to mention. For the kindness and training he had had under the Rector, he was extremely grateful, and it was chiefly due to Canon Willis that they stood *here they did to-day. There were many absent ones whom he heartily wished were present—those who were out in Flanders and other places. One of them, alas !—Charles Shepley—one of the best boys with whom he had to deal, had laid down his life. He was a thorough Christian, and be hoped all others would try and be like him. There had been several changes at St. Clement's, and a great one had taken place in their church. They should love that church, for they would not get another place like it. He'(Mr. Vickers) would not, for it was a place where they could go in joy, sorrow, or pain. The centre of the church was not the man in the pulpit, but the altar, and the centre of their religion should be He who came to them in their trouble and joy alike. Urgency of Foreign Missions.

"Two years ago," continued Mr. Vickers, "I offered myself to the Bishop of Zanzibar for mission work, but it was God's will that I should not then go. I still mean to go, how- ever, if God allows it. You should not forget the Church in India and Africa. We have sent .250 a Year to the Foreign Missions, and I hope you will keep it up. You have collected .247 already this year." Referring to the work of the future, Mr. Vickers said he knew they were glad that Mr. Armour was going to take on his (Mr. Vickers's) work. He knew they would help him in every way, and he would ask them to remember that different men had different ways of working. They should all live life to its highest, fullest, and noblest point. There were much brighter days in-front than there had been behind. They should look after the boys and girls, for upon them depended the manhood of the future.

Concluding, Mr. Vickers said he was leaving them solely for experience, and it was causing him much heart-searching. He was much more sorry than he could express. "But I must do my duty, and although we shall be divided in space, we shall not be in spirit," he said, amid applause.

The Rev. J. M. Buckmaster,. the Rev. S. G. Stanton, Mr. F. Darby, Mr. Armour and Col. Mocatta all spoke in glowing terms of Mr. Vickers's good work, and expressed regret at his departure. A suitable concert was given, the artistes being the Misses May Cross, Jessie Berry, Edith Glover and Alice Burgess, Mr. Berry, Mr. Bes- wick, and Master Neville Davies. Mr. F. Darby and Mr. E. Dell accompanied.

A Country Joke. An old bachelor, after all his abortive matri- monial attempts, exclaims :—" When I remem- ber all the girls I've met together, I feel like a rooster in the fall, exposed to every weather; I feel like one who treadi alone, some barn-yard 'all deserted, whose oats are fled, whoso hens are dead. and 6ft to rnarket started."

THE EXAMINER, S ATURDAY, MAY 27, 1916.

REV. W. AND MRS. REID.

Presentations On Their Depar-

===== 0002601_19160527_art0051.txt | included=true | included =====
ture For London.

TRIBUTE FROM TOWN AND CHURCH.

No fewer than four presentations were nuide to the Rev. W. Reid and ,Mrs. Reid at a social gatheting on Monday evening ;n the St. John's Presbyterian Church, Wilderspool-causeway. The occasion was the leave-taking of Mr. and Mrs. Reid for a new sphere of religio is activity at Trinity Church, Canonbury, London, N.

The Rev. W. Reid has been minister at St. John's for seven years, during which time the Church has made splendid progress. In his labours for its welfare, Mrs. Reid has been a constant and 'able helpmate, and by their activities and lovable characters they have gained the esteem of not only the members of their own Church, but the townspeople. Mr. Reid's activities have gone beyond the sphere of his congregation. He has taken a deep interest in the religious and social welfare of the town as a whole, and it seemed only a fitting con- clusion to his services in Warrington that his laudable work should be recognised in civic quarters. Alderman P. Peacock, the Mayor, honoured the occasion with his presence, and paid a tribute to Mr. Reid's work in the borough. • There was a large gathering of members cf the congregation and friends. Mr. W.' Russell presided, and others present were the Revs. W. T. Derry, j. J. Glover, H. Ogden, j. Paton Rogerson, Nantwich, and R. j. Wright, Bowdon.

The presentations consisted of a beautiful sitting-room clock and purse of money to the Rev. W. Reid,\ and a gold pendant, set with pearls, to Mrs. Reid. a notepaper embossing stamp from the Sunday school to Mr. Reid, and ,wbook to Mrs. Reid from the members of the British Women's Temperance Association.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0052.txt | included=true | included =====
A MINISTER OF PARTS.

On behalf of the Free Church Council, the Rev. W. T. Derry said thpy had no stauncher minister in the town during the past seven Years than the Rev. W. Reid. He commented upon the friendly spirit prevailing among the ministers of the town, and declared that if Ministers were not friendly they could net ex- pect the Churches to be. The spirit of frater- nity had something to do with the unity, of Churches more than some people thought. The congregation of St. John's had fond their minister to be a man of knowledge, and a man of experience, watchful and sincere. They had been' favoured in his ministry. (Applause.) The Chairman voiced on behalf of the congre- gation the sense of regret they felt at the de- parture of the reverend gentleman and his wife, and expressed the hope that they would find happiness and help in their new sphere of labour. During the ,seven years Air. Reid had been among thef. the Church :lad been very progressive, and they had worked together

harmoniously. The congregation 'desii(;el in some tangible way to_express their appreciation df the services Mr. and Mrs. Reid had ren- dered to the Church, and he called upon Mrs. Hawthorn and Mrs. Brown, two members of the congregation connected longest with the Church, to make the presentations. This the ladies did in it graceful way, which was followed by a third nreseiftation. made by Mr. R. Mungall, on behalf of the Sunday school staff and scholars.

Mr. Mungall said the Rev. W. Reid had taken a great interest in fostering the young life of his Church. It was, perhaps, a sail fact that there was a tendency among ministers to divorce the Sunday school from the Church. Wherever this was done it was fatal to Church life. The life of the Church and the Sunday school should be interwoven; unless this was done, no minister could hope for success, and did not deserve success. The Sunday school was the feeding stream of a Church. They admired their minister because he had fostered and nurtured the young life of his Church. (Ap- plause.) A pleasing tribute to the work Mrs. Reid had done on behalf of the British Women's Temperance Association was paid by Mrs. Owen, who handed to Mrs. Reid a sheaf a lovely lilies and a book, "The Life of Frances Willard," as a token of esteem from the ladies of the Association. Mrs. Owen said that though she was not a Presbyterian, she loved all who loved the Lord Jesus Christ.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0053.txt | included=true | included =====
REV. W. REID AND THE BOYS' HOME- COMING.

Responding on behalf of his wife and him- self, Mr. Reid said their work on behalf of the British Women's Temperance Associatipn had been gladly done. They had been in the Midst of a companionship which had been in accord- ance with their own thoughts and sentiments. They might wonder why he was going away, and where the necessity came in. He might give reasons for going and reasons for staying. Every one of the reasons whin he could advance, to his .mind, seemed swept away by the realisation of his own soul, that the hand of which he had spoken of last night 1-ad come out of the unseen, and was pointing his way for him from here to yonder. His one great pang of regret was that he would not be in Warrington when the boys came home from the Front. He would not be the minister at St. John's to welcome them. ,1-Te would give a great deal to be with them When that happy day came. They had prayed earnestly and regularly for their boys, and the answer would cetme. They would have great joy when that day came. Another reason for the sadness ef his parting was that the boys and girls of the Sumlay school, and those who attended the morning services, might have received more of his interest and companionship. The relationship between a minister and his congregation was a very peculiar one. The relations which existed between him and the souls of his people was altogether unparalleled in any other walk of life. If the relations were happy there was no happier state in the whole world. If e they were unhappy, and supposing that the man felt that he- must of necessity take up a fighting attitude, the whole fabric of the Church was injured. The man's soul became embittered, and the congregation's as well. During the seven years his wife and himself had been amongst them their relation- ship with the congregation had grown more cordial year by year. There was not an office bearer or a Sunday school teacher but whom he parted with on the most cordial terms and relationship. From the day when he and his wife came among them they had kindness on all hands.

He felt confident that the congregation would weld itself for one purpose—to secure for the Church a man of God's own choice. Their new minister would probably go al9ng different lines that he (Mr.- Reid) folloW6d. and very likely it would be good for the Church and their town. Their new minister would COMP with a newness of life, newness of thought, and newness of method, and it would be a benefit to the solid foundation which had been built at St. John's. If ever they desired him to come to St. John's for a Week-end he would do so most cordially. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19160527_art0054.txt | included=true | included =====
MAYOR EXPRESSES TOWNI, REGRET.

The Mayor, speaking on behalf of the towns- people, said the departure of Mr. Reid from Warrington was not only a severe loss to his Church, but to the town. He (the Mayor) had a great personal regard for Mr. Reid, and on all hands he had heard regret expressed at his leaving. They needed a minister more than ever now, and they would need them more when their loved ones came back from the far. They would need them in the rejoicing to wel- come back the heroes, and so let their soldiers know that the town had been looked after in the way it should be. He hoped that in his new sphere of work his usefulness would be felt just the same as• it had been in Warrington. (Applause.) The remainder of the evening was spent in social. intercourse, and refreshments were par- taken of. A pleasing vocal programme was con- tributed to by Miss Emily Taylor, Mr. Pearce, Mr. J. E. Hannan, with Mrs. Hutt-m as accom- panist. ,Letters -regretting inability to attend the preEtentation were • read from the Rev. T. T. James and the Rev. J. Yonge, who, unhappily, is unwell.

A woman seldom loses her head over a man who has lost his hair.

FLOWER DRAWING COMPETITION.

MT DEAR CHILDREN,-

As I expected, the flower-drawing compe- tition has, produced a good response and a num- ber of really artistic drawings. You will have noticed that in many cases of late our prize- winners have been new members. This week I am pleased to know that one of Uncle Ned's oldest nieces has won, and so maintained the reputation of those who formed the Corner in the,old days. There are several very clever boys and girls among those older members. The prize goes to

Niece Jenny Dunlop (No. 380), 52, Chester- road, who attends Arpley-street School. Niece Jenny also tried to send me the 800th new member, but only succeeded in getting two of the necessary three to make the number. Nephew Leo. P. Fox sent in three names, and so had the honour of supplying the 800th.- Leo is an energetic member of the Corner. Niece Lucy Hankinson sent in two new names; while the following sent in one each: Nephew Harold Whittle, Niece Lilian Himsworth, Niece Mabel Langley.

WALKING DAY!

You will all have been very glad to hear that Walking Day is to be observed this year. ' It will be slightly different from past years. Bands are very scarce because of the war, and there are few men left to carry the great banners that usu,ally make the procession so magnificent. Carters will not be available to cart the very little children through the town to the fields or railway stations; and economy makes it neces- sary that there shall be no trips by train. Last year the treat was partly dropped because it was thought-we should not enjoy ourselves while so many of our friends and relations were suffering in the trenches; but our brave-hearted soldiers .wrote home saying it was against...their wishes that the children at home should be .deprived of their enjoyment. They said, in fact, that they would be happier if they knew that the children at home were happy, and so we are to have Walking Day with all its enjoyments and as little of its expenses as possible. It, would be a good guide to the Mayor, Town Council, and the school superintendents if they knew what the children thentselves would like, so I will give next week's prize to the boy or girl who sends me the best essay on How to Observe Walking Day This Year. I shall expect to have suggestions from most of my 800 nieces and nephews, considering the im- portance of the subject. Your loving UNCLE JACK,.

WOMAN'S WORLD. .:..:. .:..:. .:„. ~.:. . • , .:. .:.

CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MARJORIE.

Lady Hardinge In India. The work of Empire building has not been accomplished by men only; women have done their part in the development of the Oversea Dominions. Thus the name of the Marchioness of Dufferin and Ava will always be gratefully remembered in India, where she was untiring in her labours for the good of the native women and children. Lady Hardinge also did a great work in India, in ameliorating suffering, ,and the Maharani of Bhannagar has paid a remark- able tribute to her memory. It is the more in- teresting from the revelation it affords of the qualities that appeal to the native feminine mind, which evidently fully appreciates the aid and help given by the English wife to her hus- band in a time of stress and difficulty. One of the greatest of the good works due to the initiative of Lady Hardinge was the establish- ment of a medical college for women, staffed by women. The magnificent college buildings, now nearing completion, will be one of the glories of the new capital of India, and another monu- ment to her womanly concern for the welfare cf the children of India, is the infants' home at Bhopal, to which she gave her name. For the British Housewife.

Thrift exhibitions are the order of the day, both in town and country, for the subject of economy has become -all-important, and is no longer a personal, but a national duty. No better way could be devised of bringing before housewives the economical use of meat, fuel, clothes; the possibilities of poultry-keeping in suburban gardens, &c., than these exhibitions. Haybox and lamp cooking, home' lapndering and carpentering, the care of money, gardening for towndfolks, and how to raise vegetables and hardy fruits, the care of infant life, are dealt with by the National Training School of Cookery, at their exhibition in London. The newest labour saving appliances are on show, and form an interesting feature of all the exhi- bitions. A number of these are most ingenious, and deserve to be widely known, and used in

servantless and other households. As the profits pf the greater number of these shows are devoted to one or other of the many war charities, they deserve to be supported by the general public, as well as by frugally minded housewives. 1 Home Economy.

If people are not saving and economising it is not for want of being shown how, and yet another attempt in this direction will be niade at Princes' Skating Club, where the National Economy Exhibition and Conference will be held from June 26th to July Bth. The club is in normal times the resort of rank and fashion, but women whose dress allowance runs into three if not four figures a year, as well as others who do not spend as many shillings, will be shown how a dainty summer frock can be fashioned out of eight yards of material at sd. per yard, and how a charming hat can be made of materials ,that need cost no more than 2s. At the dress consultation- bureau, visitors will be shown how they may dress artistically on £5, £lO or £2O a year. Also how children's clothing may be made from discarded skirts and other garments, and how a use may be found for old stockings, gloves, boots, &c. There should

be no need for a dustbin except for ashes, and another of the axioms of this up-to-date economy is that every woman should be her own handy- man, and be Able to carpentery, plumbing, and glazing, as well as intensive gardening. These exhibitions astonish even the most careful by illustrating the many unexpected ways in which they may save and he self-reliant.

Black, and Contrasting Colours. Black is to be very much worn this summer, perhaps because it is, or can be made, economi- cal, as well as smart. The woman with. an instinct for colour can, by means of ribbons or flowers, ring a dozen changes on one black gown. For instance, the lower half of a bodice, if formed of a broad floral patterned ribbon, gives relief to a gown of sable 'hue or the same result may be sought in a filmy veiling, on which the embossed flowers in vivid colourings, of ribbon, have been appliqued. For evening wear black may be saidato be thee leading shade, and when contrasted r with white, if carefully arranged, nothing iS of more distinguished appearance. The fashion for mixing several colours in a gown leads sometimes to disaster, more especially when the colours chosen are vivid. It is a mistake to mix pastel shades with others, yet this is frequently done by those devoid of the colour sense, but a clear light colour such as lemon yellow, lavender blue, or r+nrnly pink, makes an effective touch with black.

Evening Blouses and the Apron. Afternoon and evening blouses made in filmy materials such as georgette, are elaborately trimmed with openwork stitching, and picot- edged frills, and for these a delicate lilac is a very proper shade. Stripes and squares in strong contrasting colours are also favoured. Dressy blouses of this description are given hem- stitched seams, and, now and again becoming high collars that button up closely sto the throat, the cuffs almost covering the hands. Basques, of course, are well to the fore, gener- ally short, but always full. Sleeves are so various in shape that to enumerate the half would require considerable space. They may be of the bishop variety, when they are drawn in with ribbons, and end in a frill, or they may reach-only to the elbow, where they are frilled with lace. Others are of accordion-pleated chiffon. Among revivals, the apron, that is to be worn over the fashionable tea frock,' deserves nbtice. In its newest guise, this old-fashioned accessory is made of white lace, but that it will meet with much favour is very doubtful. Fashions in Bodices and Sleeves.

r The skimpiness of the bodice, as compared with the generous width of the skirt, is the pre- liminary merely to the tighter waist, ithat fashion has decreed shall be the coming mode. This necessitates a change in the setting of the sleeve, which is now put into the shoulder as in Early Victorian days, although the Raglan has by no means disappeared. A dart seam goes from the arm-hole to the waist, and this firm line causes the bodice to hug the waist, and is the latest and most approved style. This season a tendency is shown to mingle the fashions of distinct periods, in one toilette, the effect being often both quaint and picturesque. Watteau and Early Victorian fashions are not now con- sidered inconsistent with the charming Marie Antoinette fichu, and the ruffled sleeve of the time of that unfortunate Queen. By the aid of such a toilette, another scheme may have for its distinguishing feature severely simple draperies, arranged on classical lines, whilst yet another will have panniers, and a short train.

ST. BARHAM' CHURCH.

Sale Of Work in Aid Of The

Parochial Funds.

In order to swell various parish funds of the St. Barnabas' Church, a two days' sale of work was held on Wednesday and Thursday in the Patten Hall. There was a fairly large number of parish- ioners and friends present on the first day to witness Mrs. John White (late of Moore Hall) perform the opening ceremony. The Vicar (the Rev. J. H. G. Hollingworth) said that, after paying all expenses, they had left from last year's sale a sum of M. He (the vicar) had decided to divide that sum equally between the Extension Fund and Augmentation and Beneficiaries' Fund. He had had an offer from the Liverpool Aug- mentation and Beneficiaries' Fund, that if he could raise £5O they would meet it with another £lOO. This would be handed over to the Ecclesiastical Fund, with the hope that it would be met with another £l5O. By means of the interest on this sum, the living of St. Barnabas' would be inci eased from £230 to £239. They hoped by this year's sale of work they would be able to help the Extension Fund. Their hearty thanks were due to the workers, who had laboured hard to get the work to- gether, and it was wonderful to think they had done so well, considering the high prices of materials, &c. He (the vicar) was especially pleased with the refreshment stall and the Bible Class stall, which he 'did not think would have done so well, particularly the last named stall, for many members of the Bible Class had joined' the Colours.

Mrs. Ashley, Mrs. Sutton, Mrs. Kent, Mrs. Birchall, Mrs. Gleason, Misses Whitehouse, S. Whitehouse, E. Whitehouse and S. Booth. Flower Stall.—Mrs. Hands, Misses A. White- house, H. Hasall and N. Hands. Ice Cream Stail.—Misses B. and E. Broad- hurst.

Dips.—Misses L. Boyle, L. Hankinson and J. Howard.

SECOND DAY'S PROCEEDINGS.

The opening ceremony on the second day was performed by Mrs. Fox.

The Vicar announced that the proceeds of the previous day amounted to 59 ss. 6d., which in- cluded a donation of £lO from Lady Greenall. (Applause.) They were extremely thankful to receive anything from her this year, because they all knew what a good work she was doing for the soldiers, who needed the support better than they did. _ _

Mr. Hollingworth then announced that they were trying to help the soldiers and sailors by means of two draws, half the proceeds of which would go to them. Half the proceeds of the first draw, organised by the Bible Class Stall, would go to the blind soldiers and sailors, and half the proceeds of the other draw, organised by the men of the congregation, would go to the Whitecross Hospital. They hoped to raise about £lO altogether. In declaring the sale open, Mrs. Fox said it gave her great pleasure to attend and help Mr. Hollingworth and his willing people, for they were doing a splendid work. In proposing a hearty vote of thanks to Mrs. Fox, the Rev. H. V. Pigot said she had an ancestral connection with St. Barnabas' Church, a relation of hers, the late Mr. Owen, having planned the building of the church. The best thing they could do was to press the 'completion of the church—which would be a most handsome one when completed—as a means of thanking Mrs. Fox, and to the honourable memory of the late Mr. Owen.

The proceeds of the second day amounted to £7l 15s. 3d., bringing the total to £l3l Os. 9d.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0059.txt | included=true | included =====
Church Must Be Carried On.

Mr. J. E. Seeker said that at the present time they were all being called upon to economise in eyed way, and there were people who thought that sales of work should not be held. He did not hold with such views, however, for the work of the Church must be carried on, and if the national obligations were to be met at the expense of cutting down parochial charities, we were not making the sacrifices we ought to do. We must be ready for our boys when they came home. Not only must we find work to keep the parish going, but we must keep it going longer and brighter. It was our bounden duty to do so, for the boys were com- ing back with far stronger religious instincts than before. (Applause.) On the proposition of the Rev. B. Percival, seconded by Mr. Frank Webb, a vote of thanks was accorded Mrs. White. The Stan°!dors. The stallholders were : _ _

TRADES COUNCIL

AND TOWN COUNCIL DEADLOCK.

There is a deadlock between the Town Coun- cil and the Trades and Labour Council, the leading lights on the latter body having suc- ceeded, as was expected, in getting Messrs. DUKES and &ADMIX restored among the nominations for seats on the Military Pen- sions Committee. There is no possibility of the Town Council re-considering, or, in any case, altering their decision, and the select few who spoke in the name of the Trade Unions of the town would have been well advised had they bowed to the inevitable. Instead of acting wisely in this way they began to hurl asnersions about, because they form a kind of fragrant red herring to draw across the trail. For instance, Mr. CHAS. DUKES said •the men who had opposed his election on the Pensions Committee were more desirous that he should be discredited in the Labour world than that the soldiers who were to 'return should receive the generous treatment which they boasted of. One oan easily see the object of such a state- ment, and it can as easily be disproved. Most of those who have objected to the actions of those who control the Trades Council have the interests of Trade Unionism at heart, quite as much as Mr. DUKES. The Town Council consists largely of Trade Unionists and friends of the movement. The Wire- drawers and others who have seceded from the Trades Council, and others who have re- fused to join the Council "as .at present constituted," are Trade Unionists.

It seems to have -puzzled the champions of the Trades Council to find a reason for the objections of the Town Council to the nominees who were rejected. One of the principal reasons that caused the Council to refuse to accept the nominations was the reading of the following :

===== 0002601_19160527_art0072.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MILITARY IDEAL.

Young man,—The lowest aim in your life is to be a,good soldier. The good soldier never tries to distinguish right from wrong. He never thinks, he never reasons; he only obeys. If he is ordered to fire on his fellow-citizens, on his friends, on his neighbours, he obeys without hesitation. If he is ordered to fire down a crowded street when the poor are clamouring for bread, he obeys, and, seeing the grey hairs of age stained with red and the life tide gushing from the breast of woman, feels neither remorse nor sympathy. _ _ _

If he is ordered off as one Of the firing squad to execute a hero or benefactor, he fires with- out hesitation, though he knows the bullet will pierce the noblest heart that ever beat in human breast.

A good soldier is a blind, heartless, soulless, murderous machine. He is not a man, he is not even a brute, for brutes only kill in self- defence. All that is human in him, all that is divine in him, all that con- stitutes a man, was sworn away when he took the enlistment oath. His mind, his con- science, and his very soul are in the keeping of his officer. No man can fall lower than a soldier—it is the depth beneath which we cannot go. Young man, don't be a soldier or a Terri- torial. BE A MAN!

Reproduced from the Daily Herald."

That appeared in "The Labour Gazette," "the official organ of the Warrington Trades and Labour Council, guaranteed circulation 3,000 monthly., Editor: Chas. Dukes, July, 1914," or just on the eve of the outbreak of the war. It was, we believe, also pointed but that since the war began the Trades Council Executive stood " neutral " when asked to assist in drawing up a scheme for recruiting; that(they refused to assist the thrift cam- paign, and that there was a great revolt against their action when they suddenly abandoned their neutrality and sought to oppose the Military Service Bill in the name of the Trade Unionists of the town. In the meantime, they had forced a contested elec- tion—and were somewhat soundly beaten— at a time when internal peace was asked for, nationally and locally. Finally, having failed to get into the Council, Mr. DIMES accepted the situation philosophically, if not calmly, and declared that he was content not to have anything to do with the 'representatives of our "rotten borough."- The Town Council, we presume, are but taking him at his word.

We should like to draw the attention of our readers to the letter which Mr. W. Peter Rylands has addressed to us on the question
