THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MAY 27, 1916.

BACK TO THE TOWN COUNCIL.

Messrs. Dukes And Seabury Re-nominated for Pensions

Committee.

"WHAT HAVE I DONE? "-QUESTION FOR ALL.

By a large majority the Trade Unionist representatives, at a meeting at the Town Hall on Tuesday evening, protested against the re- fusal of the Town Council to accept their nominees, Messrs. Charles Dukes and A. E. Seabury as members of the War Pensions Com- mittee.

These two gentlemen headed the list of the six nominated by a similar meeting of trade unionists a few weeks ago, but for a reason which the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock) explained to them on Tuesday, they were referred back " with instructions to nominate others. Instead of doing that, however, they renominated ;the rejects, and sent the names back for the Council's acceptance.

MAYOR LEAVES NOMINATIONS ALONE. On taking his seat

The May-Or recalled that at the previous meet- ing he pointed out that it would be for the Council to accept or reject the nomination of trade union representatives. They had had a meeting lasting an hour and a quarter, and it had been decided not to appoint Messrs. Dukes and Seabury. In case of a deadlock between that meeting and the Council the matter would be submitted to the Statutory Committee in London, who would decide exactly what was to be done. He had received a letter from Mr. Edwin Plinston, president of the Trades and Labour Council, who said, "'I am extremely sorry and disappointed that the names selected and approved unanimously- by the last meeting (which was composed of repre4ntatives of ,non- affiliated as well as affiliated unions, to the Trades Council) should not be accepted by the Town Council. It appears futile to meet and nominate when those receiving the highest votes, are to. be ejected. I hope, even yet, tihe Council will agree to the last Belection." In.con- elusion, the Mayor suggested that the representit- tires should appoint their own chairman and go into the matter, afterwards' sending in 'their nominations to the Town Clerk. " Before you leave," said Mr. Harry Sinclair, as the Mayor was gathering up his papers, " I think we should know some of the reasons why they have' not been appointed. Also I regret to say that one of the approved nominees, Mr. Robert Boole, has enlisted and, therefore, some- one else will have to be appointed." The Mayor replied that the Council by a large ' majority believed that two of the gentlemen nominated were not the right people to be on the committee, being of opinion that those who sent their sons to fight would not like Mr. Dukes and Mr. Seabury to deal with their cases should they. require assistance on their, return.

them. Within his own family there were men who had made sacrifices in the war, but he de- murred to preaching patriotism by proxy. What he meant by that was: if he had a brother who had fought during the war and a father who had worked on munitions at double the age of some who were boasting, that should not be placed to his (the speaker's) credit. The ques- tion for them all should be, "What have I done?" Mr. Baker: That's it !

Mr. Dukes: Well, I will tell you. Years prior to the war I was interested in those political organisations which have specialised upon international difficulties, and I was part of the general Labour movement which agreed to a war programme._ __ _

Mr: Baker: Mr. Chairman, I shall ask you to limit the speeches. Mr. Dukes: You may be afraid that I am going to upset your theories. , Mr. Baker: I am not afraid of anything you say.

Mr. Dukes: You are a representative of an industry which has sacrificed nothing. There is no dilution in the wire-drawing trade. Mr. A. Siddall (Wiredrawers' Society): I beg yours. There are fifteen per cent. of our mem- bers fighting with MM. Forces. Mr. Dukes: Thi• statement I am making is that those men have no more gone because they were wiredrawers than ours have gone because they were gas workers and general labourers. As wiredrawers you have not made any sacrifice at all. Your rates and conditions have 'been maintained. You have worked Upon war re- quirements equally with other industries in which trade union privileges have been sacri- ficed, and there are men on the Town Council who are representative of interests who express their patriotism in the lione of a return of five per cent.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0075.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. ELWELL TAKES THE CHAIR.

No Dividing Line 01 Labour.

Bowing politely, the' Mayor then left the Chamber, and Mr. J. Elwell was immediately voted to the chair.

Having expressed his regret thatit had been found necessary to hold that meeting, Mr. Elwell thought they ought to go into the whole problem and be properly satisfied before they separated. He thought, however, it would be better if they did not lay down anything about the Trades Council or non-Trades Council mem- bers. That barrier should not enter into the question at all. There shouT.: be no dividing line in the Labour ranks. Mr. H. Sinclair said they ought to raise a strong protest against the Town Council being the deciding factor. It as not to be expected that the men chosen to be the delegates of Labour should represent either capitalism or the Trades Council, 'and they ought not to he thrust lightly on one side. Messrs. Dukes and Seabury were well fitted to ,judge in such cases as would come before the committee, as 2,800 of the local members of their union were in the Forces, and the representatives had to deal with such cases day by day. It was a farce to call trade union representatives together to select men if they were to be thrown out be- cause two or three individuals did not care for them. That meeting ought to refuse to allow any other names to go forward. (Applause.) _ _ _ _ .

Mr. F. A. Bakei, of the Wirdrawers' Society, said that at their last meeting they did not an- ticipate any' trouble, but they could quite understand why the trouble had arisen. The Trades Council took a ;course of action which did not meet with the approval of a great many people in Warrington;tit did not meet with the approval of the Wiredrawers' Society. The Council refused to give any help in recruiting or to do anything to, further the objects of the Government in trying to win the war. The Town Council no doubt thought that, on that account, they should take no part in anything appertaining to pensions or to anything con- nected with the men who had gone to the Front.

He could say many things about people who were doing their patriotism by proxy, but he did not want to enter into them unless his own attitude was to be discussed, Resuming, Mr. Baker said he was desirous that no men should be put on the committee who would cause friction and trouble, and he believed that men who had opposed anything in connection with the war would he likely to cause trouble. Mr. Dukes: I think You ought to name them. Mr. Baker: I am not working my patriotism by proxy. I have a son fighting in France. Mr. Dukes: Don't proceed by innuendo. If the Council persisted in rejecting the names let them go to the Statutory Committee, where no personal spleen could be introduced and where qualifications would be the test.

WHAT HAS MR. DUKES DONE?

Mr. Baker: I rise to a point of order. We have not tome here to hear long addresses. We never said the wiredrawers had sacrificed any- thing. Just as you say I have made no sacri- fice, so I tell you that you have not. Mr. Baker: I have done something to help the country. You have done nothing. _ _

Mr. Dukes :You don't know wfrat I have done. The fact is you can't take your gruelling. Mr. Baker lighted a cigarette. The Chairman: Don't let us have cross-table talk.

Mr. Dukes, Continuing, said that as to his position in the recruiting campdign, he con- sidered the qupstion of enlistment was a matter of life and death. He had never assumed the position of telling a man what he ought to do in matters of that kind, and he disagreed with any man who claimed the right to dictate what another ought to do in matters of life and death. For that reason he refused to assist in recruiting. If he had been instrumental in per- suading any man to sacrifice his life he could not have looked his (the soldier's) parents in the face again. And yet that was what he was asked to do when he was asked to recruit. if he had persuaded a man to enlist and he had lost a leg he (Mr. Dukes) would have regarded that as a life's responsibility. What was really at stake in the present dispute—and it was no use burking it—was this: The men who had opposed his election on the committee ,were more desirous that he should be discredited in the Labour world than that the soldiers who were to return should receive the generous treatment which they boasted of.

A BACK ANSWER.

Mr. Baker: That is nonsense.

GOSSIP.

Mr. W. M. Hughes, Prime Minister of Australia, came to Warrington yesterday morn- ing unhonoured and unsung. Along with his party he was travelling on the 9.55 through the Central Station from Liverpool to Manchester, where he has been acclaimed of the people.

Few knew of his coming to Warrington—a friend,. .of mine, myself, ancl, of course, Alderman Bennett. We had. no difficulty in espying the beautiful saloon in which the party were housed. At one end Mr. Hughes' secretaries were relishing pipes and popular magazines, Mr.i. Hughes sat mid- way reading a newspaper, the nurse had her hands full with the baby, who was trying to rub its dainty nose against the window. Apparently, the youngster was the only one in- terested in Warrington.

But where was Mr. Hughes? The Alderman was getting anxious. So we moved along, and by putting our faces full against, a window saw him lying on his back in the compartment of the saloon, thinking out his MaAchester speech.

He had his jacket off, used his coat for a quilt, And one could see his blue and white shirt sleeves as he raised his hand to keep his cigarette alight. He looked rather tired. His tall hat and umbrella were taking a rest on the rack.

With fon/. blinds .down all was peaceful twilight within, and the P.M. was net easily discerned-. from without: . We waved our hands to him, and ha waved his cigarette and smiled pleasantly in return. Then he looked up at the ceiling to resume his meditation; and we retired.

We were all Mr. Hughes saw of Warrington. His baby at the window was the only sightseer among the party, and perhaps when it grows to talking age it will tell its illustrious father what its impressions were.

In getting Alf. Leonard and Elven tic:ll;es, of the "Step This Way " revue, to appear at the Blinded Heroes' matinee at the Palace and Hippodrome on Thursday, Mr. Jackson has garnered some particularly good talent into his net—or, rather, on to his stage. These notable comedians will be performing in Manchester during the week, and, if at all possible, will make the journey, returning as soon as they have finished their "turns" and the applause has died down.

When in Warrington a few weeks ago they told me they make it a point of duty to con- tribute to matinees in • aid of -war and allied funds whenever they are asked.

A few weeks ago the town was honoured by a visit from the 'North-Western Federation of the Ancient Order of Foresters. .

To-day we shall be further honoured by the holding here, for the first time, of the Indepen- dent Order of Oddfellows (M.U.) Lancashire and adjacent districts' conference. The Mayor will welcome the delegates at the Town Hall, at 2.45, and at 3 they -will proceed with the con- sideration of the programme of the A.M.C. at Bristol in Whit-week.

On niembers' of Mr. Arthur Down's Band presenting themselves for the weekly practice on Wednesday evening at his lthme in Stockton Heath they-. saw displayed under the clock a notice: "This clock is at the old Greenwich time," and Mr. Down intimated that he is not going to alter it. Neither does he intend ad- vancing the fingers of his watch.

It is as well to follow "common time" when the music of the land is set to it.

The Warrington Parish Church Magazine announces that the purse of gold presented to the Rev. T. Vickers contained £so—" a noble tift by worthy givers to a worthy recipient," it adds.

At tho Warrington Tribunal this week most of those who were conditionally exempted signi- fied their willingness to join the Volunteer Training Corps, and Mr. Bradbury, a member, remarked: "Soon we shall have the Park full." That. battalion we have been waiting for should soon he formed, if they all keep their promise. I think they will. Englishmen have a keen sense of honour.

NO SMALLPDX.

The Mayor Says No Truth In Startling Rumours.

UNNECESSARY ALARM.

We are requested by His Worship the Mayor to state that after very careful inquiries into the alleged outbreak of smallpox in this town he is assured by the Medical Office, of Health that no such case exists and that the town is entirely free from that fell disease. His Worship is most anxious to appease the public mind, which he regrets has been un- necessarily alarmed. _

We have ascertained that the condition of quarantine among the Military Forces is not limited to Warrington, but applies to the whole of South-West Lancashire, and will remain in force for military reasons throughout that dis- trict until the 31st of this month.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0082.txt | included=true | included =====
OSWALD GARSIDE, R.I.

Warrington Student Who Has Had Remarkably Successful Career.

Warringtonians will learn with pride that Mr. Oswald Garside has been elected an "R.l." that. is a Member of the Royal Institute of Painters in Water Colours, an election that is subject to His Majesty's approval. It is the highest distinction that c,an be bestowed upon an artist in this particular branch of. work. The holders of the title are limited in number to 100.

Mr. Garside is the son of the late Mr. Thomas Garside, chemist, of Southport, and Mrs. Gar- side, of Stockton Heath, and a brother of Mr. F. Garside, of the firm of Garside and Jolley, printers, Warrington. He came to Warrington when about twelve years of age, and was subse- quently apprenticed to cabinet-making with Messrs. Garnett, of Penketh. When out of his time he went to the Warrington School of Art, studied with a keenness that foretold success, and gained a Lancashire County Connell Scholarship, which took him for three years to Paris, where he studied at the well-known Julien's Academy, under Jean Paul Laurens and other famous French masters. His progress was so marked that he was given a twelve months' extension of the scholarship, and the additional time was spent in Venice. Coming home, he spent a couple of years experimenting before he entered upon any serious work. Then he rapidly established his worth, became a regular exhibi- tor at the Royal Academy, the Royal Institute of Painters in Water Colours, the Paris Salon, and the various exhibitions, including the one held a few years ago at Liverpool. Now, at the age of 46, he finds himself classed with the select few at the very top of his profession. He re- sides at Barnes, London.

There are two or three pictures by Mr. Garside in the Warrington Museum, and naturally there are a good many privately cwned by. local ad- mirers of his work.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0083.txt | included=true | included =====
VOLUNTEERS.

Needed In Case Of Possible German Invasion.

A REAL DANGER.

To the Editor of "The Examiner."

Sir,—On Saturday last Sir James de Hoghton, the County Commandant, visited Warrington in order to inspect the Warrington and Widnes Companies of the South-West Lancasaire Volunteers.

He expressed himself well pleased, with the bearing and smartness of. the men but was disappointed with their small number...

The War Office have issued orders tl at the Volunteer Corps must be organised for the defence of the main lines of railway:. in the event of invasion, when enemy agents would undoubtedly make every endeavour to interfere with the movement of troops. All in authority are of opinion that the Germans will make a serious endeavour to invade Great Britain, and the less encouraging their progress on the Con- tinent the more likely they will be to make the attempt:

===== 0002601_19160527_art0084.txt | included=true | included =====
Would Be Lamentable.

The present number of the Warrington Corps available is insufficient for the purpose, and it would he lamentable if we have to advise the War Office that Warrington men are unable to face the small sacrifice involved in offering this amount of service to their country, and I con- fidently appeal to the men of Warrington to come forward and join. Sir James was saying on Saturday that Great Britain did not know what war is. They have vever experienced the horrors of war them- selves, and I am sure he is right, and that the men of this country do not fully realise the terrors against which they are being protected by our Army and Navy, and what would be their fate and the fate of their womenfolk should that defence fail.

Numbers must stop, at home owing to age, or for the purpose of necessary. duties, but it is difficult to justify an arbitrary, arrangement allotting hardship, discomfort and peril to some, and ease, comfort and safety to others. Surely the men who are privileged to stay at home should at least show their readiness to help, should serious emergency to the State require it.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
Disastrous Cocksuredness.

It would be a great encouragement to the men at the Front if they felt that the men at the back had a better appreciation of the realities, and were preparing themselves to play their part should necessity arise. Our cocksuredness has frequently landed us in trouble in the past and one, day will cer- tainly bring us to final disaster, just as the same cocksuredness has caused the downfall of all the Empires in the past. A strong Warrington Battalion of Volunteers would show that Warrington, at all events, was determined not to slack, and was willing to provide the force required to fulfil the responsible duties now demanded by the War Office.

The Government have found a difficulty in equipping the Volunteer Force owing to the pressure for equipment for our huge army, and this may have been some source of discourage- ment to the movement. I am satisfied a suffi- cieney of rifles could be obtained by purchase, and if the men are forthcoming I am confident I would be able to appeal with success for funds to defray the cost of any equipment in reason required to make the Corps a credit to the town.

Recruits can be enrolled most conveniently at the Territorial Quarters on Mondays and Thursdays, at 8 o'clock, or by sending their names to Mr. P. H. Swallow, 9, Egypt-street, Warrington.—Yours faithfully, W. PETER RYLANDS. Area Commandant. Massey Hall, Thelwall, Cheshire. 25th May, 1916.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
SOLDIER-VOCALISTS.

The[wall Heys Hospital Benefits By Concert.

A spirit of happiness permeated the atmosphere of the Latchford Parish Room on Thursday night, when a concert arranged by the wounded soldiers of Thelwall Heys Hospital attracted a crowded audience.

Appreciative of the care and kindness accorded to them, the men who are at present receiving treatment at the hospital, arranged the ooncert, and it was gratifying to them to have their efforts so handsomely rewarded. It is expected that between £24 and £25 will be available for the support of the institution. There are many vocal and musical soldiers stationed at the hospital, and they gave the public an idea of their abilities in this direc- tion, the programme being almost wholly contributed by them. Patriotic, sentimental and comic songs were given, to the evident de- light of the assembly, who were particularly enthusiastic over the rendering of "When the Boys Come Home," by Miss Norton, a lady gifted with a charming voice, a number of soldiers joining in with gusto, and at the same time the Union Jack was unfurled. Miss 8100 l also gave pleasure with vocal items, and a sketch, " Mutanical Jane," played by Private Molineux, Private Batty and Miss Blood kept everyone in a merry mood. Miss Mabel Fair.- dough acted ably as accompanist, and the duties of stage manager were capably fulfilled by Lance-Corporal Ingham. Mr. and Mrs. Prost, Grappenhall Hall, took a kindly interest in the concert, and tende:ed thanks to Dressrs P. Walker ,nd Son ltd for decorating the room, and to Mr. Crompton, the staff, and soldiers for helping to make the function a success. Mr. Frost announced that arrangmeents were being made for the holding of a Whist drive in the grounds of the hospital on Whit Monday. A bouquet was presented to Miss Norton, and chocolates and cigarettes were handed round to the soldiers. Refreshments were also served by the hospital staff.

On Tuesday Mrs. Jefferson, of Gatesgarth, Latchford, held a sale of work on behalf of the hospital, and she has kindly handed over the profits, £6 13s. 6d., to Mrs. Frost.

The usual Pound Day on Thursday was very successful, gifts in money and kind amounting to over £ll.

'A MONSTRE PROGRAMME.'

Big Demand for Tickets FOr

Blinded Warriors' , Matinee.

Big things are going to happen in Warrington on, Thursday afternoon, when the matinee in aid of the Blinded Soldiers' and Sailors' Hostel will take place at the Palace and Hippodrome. The tickets have gone exceedingly well, and 'the porter has been looking up his notice board, "Standing room only." To those who want to get in had better apply for their tickets at once. Mr. Jackson, the manager, has been active in getting together a galaxy of professional music- hall talent, palatably mixed with the band of the South Lancashire Regiment, Miss Marie Houghton, and Mr. J. Babbitt. In all, there will be ltwenty items, and it is almost certain that two of the best numbers will• be provided by Alf. Leonard and Elven Hedges, of "Step This Way " fame. A delightful programme in a good cause! We wish success for the matinee. Along with Mr. Jackson, Mrs. Henry Greenall, Mr. J. H. J. Hampson have been particularly hard workers in its organisation. Its patrons are the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock), Sir A. H. Crosfield, Bart., Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart., Mr. Harold Smith, M.P., and Colonel Moggridge—all united in charity of the first order. A more pitiable plight than being blinded by shell-fire is 'unconceivable, and we are proud the people of Warrington intend sending a handsome sum to their aid.

Births and Deaths.

There were 19 more births than deaths in, Warrington in the week ended May 20th, the births numbering 40 and the deaths 21. Tho new babies were equally divided between the sexes-20 of each.

One of the deaths was due to diphtheria.

Infectious Disease:

Last week the following new cases of in- fectio-us disease were notified —Scarlet fever, 6; diphtheria, 1; tuberculous phthisis, 8; measles, 7. In the hospital are the following cases :-- Scarlet fever. 36; enteric fever, 2; diphtheria, 6; phthisis, 21.

Railway Clerks in Conference.

The Railway Clerks' 'Association was in con- ference at Cardiff last week-end and the War- rington branch, with a membership of 215, was represented by a delegate. The strength of the Warrington branch has greatly increased during the last year or two. Six years ago there were only fifteen members. At the present time they have lady members exceeding that figure, and the committee reports "And we have still more to come."

Leather Trade Activity. Unprecedented actWity prevails here, says "The Leather World," and in spite of the Con- tinued enlargement of tanneries which has been so noticeable for the past few years, it is ex- tremely difficult to cope with the amount of business passing. Tanners are workEing whole- heartedly with the authorities in producing the kind of leather mOst required, and willingly vary their production at every call from head- quarters. Thus the old conservatism has passed and the "quick-change artist taken its place. There is the huge order for high-legged Cossack boots following on the present Russian order, which must not be overlooked in esti- mating future values and supplies.

The Late Mr. J. Partington. The remains of the late Mr. Joseph Parting- ton, of Bold-street, Warrington, were interred on Tuesday in the Great Budworth Churchyard. The deceased, who died at his residence on. Satufday, was a member of an old Cheshire family, which can be traced as far back as 1200. Mr. Partington, who was 65 years of age, had been in ill-health for some months. Ile had been in business in Warrington and sur- rounding district for many years. He was a Conservative and a prominent Freemason, being a Past Master of the "De Tabley" Lodge, Knutsford; also the "Lodge of Lights," War- rington. He leaves a widow, two sons and three daughters.

- Next Week's Great Garden Party. Over 2,000 wives and widows of soldiers and sailors have accepted the invitation of the Mayor and Mayoress (Alderman and Mrs. Peter Peacock) to the garden party in Bank Park to-day (Saturday) week. Given fine weather, the event should, be an immense success. The host and hostess are in a. generous mood and their guests may rely upon having a splendid time. Refreshments are to be served, and the Band of the South Lancashire Regiment is 'to play selections, in addition to which there will also be the attraction of Mr. W. W. Whittle's Troupe, known as "The Scarlet Poms." From 2.30 until 6.30 the wives and widows, with their host and hostess, will have everything to them- selves and then the general public will be ad- mitted to share in their social pleasure.

Baptist Sunday School Anniversary

Services.

In connection with Golborne-street Baptist Sunday School the anniversary services were held on Sunday, when the Rev. R. D. Darby, of Leigh., preached both morning and evening to good congregations, and said that the only effec- tual teacher is the one who has learned in his own life the truth he is trying to apply, and, like his Master, practises it in the• sphere in which he moves, This is a lesson hard to learn, but it is a trith that all history confirms. It had its full demonstration in Jesus Himself, Who, in order to save, gave His life, and those who would be His followers must in their own way give if they would gain. In the afternoon a musical service was held, presided over bY Mr. Thos. Dale. The Rev. P. meson spoke on the decline in the attendance et Sunday schools, and the increase (pf juvenile crime,nd suggested that the former might /VI some deasure be responsible for the latter. The choir and scholars rendered special music at each service, under the leadership of Mr. II- Barnes, with Mr. S. Rickers as organist. EMPIRE DAY.

The Sane Imperialism Of The

Schools.

YOUNG WARRINGTON TRUE

Per the first time in its short history Empire bey was on Wednesday officially recognised by the Government. The fact gave an added enthusiasm to the celebrations held all over the country. In London flags waved in the gentle breeze, not only in almost every street, but from the Houses of Parliament. There was no ?stentatious display of Union Jack rosettes, but le a hundred different ways the solidarity of the Empire was emphasised.

GRAIIIIIA.R. SCHOOL.

Visit Of Officer From The Headquarters

Staff.

Major G. C. II: Wheler, I.Q.M.S. of the Head- quarters Staff of the Western Command and M.P. for the Faversham Division of Kent, _visited the Warrington Grammar • School on 'Wednesday, inspected the remarkably fine Cadet Corps possessed by the school, and then ad- d-re, ssed the boys on the Empire and its privi- legeS and responsibilities. The Corps, about 70 strong, was under the command of -Captain • W. Pearson, with Lieutenant J. Pape and • Company-Sergeant-Major L. J. Witts. On the Parade ground, and in the school afterwards, were Alderman Smethurst, one of the governors, and Mr. Vere Davies, clerk to the governors. _The Rev. Horace Gray, in calling upon Major Wheler to address the boys, referred to men horn this district who, like Colonel Greene, had Jnade the great sacrifice for the Empire. Major Wheler congratulated the boys upon their steadiness and soldierlike bearing, and expres3ed the confident belief that they would acquit themselves creditably to themselves, their school and their country when the time came for titeDA to join His Majesty's Forces. He then Passed in review the part played by the Dominions in the war, from the way in which the Canadians saved the Empire ',)y with- standing the first German surprise gas-attack, to the way in which South Africa is clearing the great sub-continent of German influence. The -Empire, he said, was on its trial and we de- pended upon the steadiness, determination and the stubbornness of the British race to see us through. Whenever the boys he was speaking to were called upon to take a serious part in the work of the Empire they must' be true to the clear duty which the Empire imposed upon' them. (Applause.) Alderman Smethurst, in proposing a vote of . thanks to Major Wheler, mentioned that the school had provided four men who had taken a prominent part in the war. The Vote was carried with ringing cheers. SECONDARY SCHOOL.

Kr. JAMES' SCHOOL.

The Reason Why We Are Filled With

Pride.

The niorning session at St. . James' Mixed School was chiefly devoted to lessons which illustrated the history, extent, population, in- dustries and resources of the Empire. In the afternoon the Headmaster (Mr. Stans- field) addressed the children, who, at intervals, sang • suitable. Patriotic songs. He showed that Empire Day meant more to Us this year than ever, because the war had revealed the unity and loyalty of our vast Empire. It was not the thought that our Empire occupied one-fifth of the surface of the globe and contained one-fourth of the population Which filled us with pride, but the fact that thousands of our fellow-subjects from across th 3 Seas had willingly come forward to help us and save us from disaster. This loyalty and Patriotism was the result of justice, which is the chief feature of British government. Mr. Stansfield referred to the fact that this Year was the tercentenary of the death of Shakespeare, perhaps the greatest Englishman .who ever lived, and he quoted many extracts from his works, showing the great poet's idea Of true patriotism.

" He which hath no stomach to this fight, Let him depart," , indicates what he would expect from the -conscience of a true Englishman, and other Passages from "Henry V." seemed particularly appropriate to the present struggle. The King's prayer before battle, and his wish to give thEroredit of victory to God are exampl:s Ive should follow:

pritain Stands For Liberty Throughout

The World. , The whole of the 400 boys attending Evelyn- street Council School marched from the play- ground at nine a.m. into the open road, and Past the flag in quadruple file, saluting within h4l usual paces of the flag-staff. °n assembling in the Central Hall (im- -I;lediately after prayers) the Headmaster (Mr. Appleton) addressed them for twenty minutes °ll the significance of their action. COULD NOT HAVE SHOT HIMSELF.

Dr. R. T. McGeagh, who was called to the sergeants' sleeping quarters, stated: I found the dead body on the floor. I found a circular wound in the chest, with blood flowing from the, mouth and nostrils. I ,could not find the exit of the bullet until after the removal ef the body to the mortuary, when, along with Dr. Symes, I found the exit at the back, about two inches from the spine. There was no mark of burning. which would have been there if the man had shot himself. Moreover, he could not have shot himself from the position of the wound., Death would, take place in less than a minute.

The Coroner asked Mr. Kelly if he wished to call his client at this stage. Mr. Kelly; No, sir. THE VERDICT.

The jury retired, and returned after six minutes' absence, when Mr. J. H. Kelly, fore- man of the jury, announced the verdict as follows: .

"The jury is unanimous in finding a verdict of wilful murder against John Williams." The accused who sat unmoved throughout the whole of the proceedings, heard the verdict an- nounced in the same stoical manner.

WYCLIFFE SCHOOLS. •

Empire Day to the scholars at Wycliffe Schools ' meant a red letter day. Each class received in- struction about the making of our mighty Empire, and the Union Jack figured largely in the programme of the day. Wycliffe is for- tunate in possessing the largest central hall of any school in the town. The various classes vied with each other in decorating their part of the hall, and the result was very pleasing and effective. The Union Jack and the flags of our Allies decorated the walls, and bunting streamed across the room. The rose bowl won by the girls for their prowess at rounders and filled with flowers displaying the national colours, occupied a place of honour in the centre of the hall, and near by stood one of the youngest and smallest girls—Bessie Fellows—holding a large Union Jack. To the singing of "When the Boys Come Home" the scholars marched into the hall, saluted the flag, and then took their allotted places. When all were assembled eager expectancy gleamed on every face, for each class was to provide an item to the afternoon's entertainment.

In addition to the national and part songs, the following children took part in the pro- gramme :—Edna Beaumont, recitation, "A Song for England "; Edith Buck, recitation, "Red, White and Blue "; Selina Hill, recitation, "A Private of thP. Buffq "; Lillian Buck, recitation,

"The Empire of our Dreams" (Alderman Ben- nett's poem); Annie Sullivan, song, "The Rosy Morn "; Harry Tatlock, song, "The Mother- land "; James Pearson and Albert Rowlinson. duet, "The Maple Leaf." Miss M. Swindells very effectively rendered the appropriate song, "Empire Day," the chorus of which was heartily entered into by the children. During the interval the Headmaster (Mr. Sydney W. Jeffery) delivered an address on Empire Day and the significance of the national flag with regard to duty, sacrifice, responsibility and obedience. After the singing of the National Anthem there were lusty cheers for his Majesty the King, the old boys of the school roll of honour, and a ringing cheer for the teachers, Messrs. Burtt, Cornall and Sinclair, who are serving with the Colours. ,

OUR LADY'S SCHOOL, LATCHFORD.

Mimic Royal Review—And Great Loyal Demonstration.

The celebration of Empire Day at Our Lady's School this year took the form of a mimic "royal review" in the 'school playground. At p.m. the head teacher (Miss A. Rourke) addressed the children on the meaning of Em- pire Day celebration, impressing upon them the necessity of being determined to grow up worthy of the great sacrifices which many of their fathers and brothers were now making for them, Sc) that in a few years' time, when they were men and women, they would be able to inherit and inhabit this great British Empire. At 3 p.m., the various classes, representing troops from a British colony or country, and wearing suitable uniforms, were marched into position by an officer (Master Lance Connor), each child saluting the Union Jack. The girls contributed a very pretty tableau, "Britannia," with "Wales," "Ireland," "Scotland," and " Italy " in attendance.

After the review by the "'royal party" (Stan- dards VI. and VII.), an open-air concert was given, consisting of patriotic songs and recita- tions, national dances, and concluding with the National Anthem Cheers were given for the King and Queen, our soldiers kind sailors, and the flag of the Empire.

CAMP TRAGEDY.

The Story Of Colour-Sergeant Malings' Death.

WILLIAMS COMMITTED.

The Knockaloe Alien Detention Camp tragedy, reported at length in "The Examiner" last week, has had a sequel this week. It will be, remembered that Colour-Sergeant William Henry Malings, 46 years of age, a married hairdresser, of 269, Liverpool-road, Warrington, while on service as guard in the Alien Detention Camp, was shot dead. Sergeant John Williams, of the same com- pany, of the Second 4th South Lancashires, was arrested in connection with the affair. It was stated that Williams was a Liverpool man, but in a letter found upon him, addressed to Mrs. Williams, the address, 14, Ship-yard, Warring- ton, was given. We proceeded to make in- quiries at this address, but found the house empty, in which state a neighbour said it had been for some time.

Against Sergeant John Williams, 47 years of age, a Coroner's Jury found a verdict of "Wilful murder," and yesterday he was com- mitted for trial.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====
ISLE OF MAN PROCEDURE.

Isle of Man law is somewhat different from the English law. The prisoner will next ap- pear before a Deemster and jury of six, to determine if there is sufficient evidence'to send him for trial. In that case the last act would be trial .at _General Gaol Delivery some weeks hence. At this Court three judges preside—the Clerk of the Rolls and the two Deemsters. The Lieutenant-Governor (Lord Raglan) is ex-officio a member of the Court. He is at present (and has been for some time) in London, unwell. The duties of Governor in his absence are per- formed by the Clerk of the Rolls, who has been appointed Deputy-Governor.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0112.txt | included=true | included =====
INQUEST RESUMED.

Curious Statements By Williams—

And A Letter.

The inQuest on the bddy of the late Colour- Sergeant Malings was resumed at the Peel Court House on Monday afternoon, Mr. 11. P. Kelly now representing Sergeant Williams. Mr. R. B. Moore appeared for the Crown. Joseph Madigan, special assistant of the com- mandant at the camp, said that when he' entered the hut where theshot had taken place he found the dead body of Sergt. Millings. A walking- stick was lying beside the deceased, near Ins hand. Malings suffered from some affliction, which affected his walking; that was Nay he used the stick. Witness searched Williams's ammunition pouch, which was hanging at the head of his cot. It should have contained ten rounds of ammunition—two clips each contain- ing five cartridges. It contained nothing. A clip containing five live cafiridges was handed to Sergt. John Sanger by Williams, who took it from his tunic pocket. There were also two live cartridges on his bed, and a spent cartridge ly:ng on the floor at the foot of the adjoining cot to Williams's. In Williams's trousers' pocket witness found two more live cartridges. That accounted for the whole ten. In Williams's trousers' pocket he found a letter, which stated: Dear Rose,—Please excuse me; I died in a good cause. Men to men. God bless you. Mrs. Williams, 14, Ship Yardi Warrington.

On the other side of the notepaper was written:

Twenty years' service with men, not a (word here indecipherable, apparently " tutor," but possibly "traitor.") Sending expert of armes. (Some more words indeci- pherable, probably a signature). I_ enclose

ei4 to Mrs. Williams, NO. 14, Ship Yard, off Bridge-street, Warrington. When he entered the room Williams was seated at the foot of his cot on a box. He had spec- tacles on reading some printed matter and smok- ing a pipe.

In reply to Mr. R. B. Moore, witness said he* had taken the height of MaHugs, and, judging from the fact that the bullet passed through the top of his chest and the height of the broken window, be was of opinion that the bullet was fired at a lower plane than, the height at which the wound was made. The nature of the hole in the glass shoved that the bullet was fired from the extreme far end of the building froiq the door near where Williams's bed was. •

By Mr. Kelly: He did not think the hole in the window had been made by a stone.

Did you notice anything about the appearawe of the prisoner?—lle had a peculiar expression of face, which one would not associate as a rule with one of ordinary intelligence.

Sergeant Stoll was called: but it was stated that he had gone across with the body and had not returned.

The Coroner said that he particularly wanted Sergt. Stoll's evidence.

"THIS IS THE CARBINE THAT DID IT."

Lance-Sergeant James Slingsby Duncan Clair said

'On getting in the room I, saw Malihgs' body on the ground, lying on his back. I saw at once he was dead. Williams was against his own cot. Vie was then filling his pipe. My cot is next but one to Williams's. Williams did not say anything at this time, My carbine was lying on my bed and Williams's was lying on his bed. Two colour-sergeants came in and one said 'Which is the carbine that did it?' Williams said 'Find out,' and a minute or two after said 'This is the carbine that did it,' and he pointed to his own, which was lying on his bed. There was no quarrelling or cross words in the Sergeants' Mess." •

By the Coroner: He did not know of any ill- feeling between the two men and he had been with them in the same room for seven months.

By Mr.'Kelly: Williams began to walk about —a most unusual demeanour. It was not his usual habit to smoke when an officer was about.

Corporal Unsworth said he had known both men for eleven months, and there was no unpleasantness between them that he knew of. Private Brown, who also rushed to the room on hearing the shot, said Sergeant Williams was standing by his cot. He was the only man there. He did not say anything, but looked dazed. "I have known both men," concluded the witness, "and I know of no reason why one should damage the other."

===== 0002601_19160527_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
"I AM SATISFIED."

Provost Captain Wm. Phillips.deposed that on Monday afternoon, while he was watching over Williams, the latter said: "I have rid of the bastard. I am satisfied." He made the state- ment voluntarily. _ _ _ _

By Mr. Kelly: Only the accused and I were present. This was about twenty minutes past five—about two hours after the occurrence. Wil- liams was sitting down about three yards from me. That was the only remark he made.

A SMART SERGEANT.

Lieut. Allen stated that on receiving a report that a man had shot himself he went to the ser- geants' sleeping quarters. The man who was shot had a stick in his hand. The ferrule was in his hand. He was struck at. once that the Inan did not shoot himself. Witness exautined a carbine lying on a cot on the right, but found it had not been used. One of the N.C.O.'s took a rifle off the other cot and said, "This is tie one." One could tell it had been discharged within an hour. There was an empty cartridge case on the floor on the right on Williams's cot, under the next cot. Witness asked who took the empty cartridge out of the breach, and Sergt. Williams, who was smoking his pipe, said, " did it." There were only two carbines lylng about.

Mr. Moore: Did you hear anyone ask whose rifle it was?

Witness: Someone said, "There is the one that did it," but I could not swear who said it. By Mr. Kelly: I told Williams to take the pipe out of his mouth when speaking to an officer. 'Williams seemed to be in a dazed con- dition. Knowing what a smart sergeant he was, it struck me as peculiar that he spoke to me with a pipe in his mouth. He always had the appearance of a smart sergeant. I know Malings, but do not know what he suffered from.

ALIVE AND WELL.

Private Ireland Not Killed, But

A Prisoner.

Gloom and sadness caused by the official an- nouncement from the War Office that the bread- winner of a house in Warrington had been killed in action has been changed into happi- ness by the receipt of a. letter from the husband stating that he is alive and well. There is no happier • family in the borough than • Mrs. Ireland and her 'seven young chil- dren, residing at 77, HaydOck-street. • Some three weeks ago Mrs. Ireland received informa- tien from the War Office that her husband, Private john P. Ireland, of tlic West Ridinv, Regiment, had been killed in action on the 26t11 April. A letter was also received from Captain Malcolm, Robertson • confirming the sad intelli- gence._•_ • • _

On Saturday, however, the post brought tho unhappy woman a letter from her "dead " husband and, naturally, her sadness quickly gave way to joy. The epistle .was very brig. but Privaite Ireland stated that he was captured while attending to wounded, 'and is now a prisoner of war in Germany, where the letter was posted. There is no doubt that, Private Ireland is quite safe and alive.

LANCE-CORPORAL B. PARSONAGE.

Royal Engineer Dangerously Wounded

In The Neck.

News has reached Mrs. Parsonage, 100, Gorsey-lane, that her husband, Lance-Corporal Benjamin Parsonage, ol.! the Royal Engineers, has been dangerously wounded. A telegram received on 'Monday, ran :—" Regret to inform you that officer commanding 10th Stationary Hospital, reports 65832 Lance-Corporal B. Par- sonage, Royal ,Engineets, is dangerously ill with a gunshot wound in the neck. Regret also that permission to visit cannot be granted." Mrs. Parsonage has received A postcard from

her husband, who says that the wound is very painful.

- Lance-Corporal Parsonage, who is 38 years of age, enlisted on February 2nd of last year. He was previously employed as a joiner by Messrs. Peter Walker and Son, Ltd., at their Dallam- lane 'Brewery. He was educated at Orford School, and attended Orford Church. He was also an Orford "old boy."

PRIVATE H. DITCHFIELD.

Killed By Shell Whilst Sitting Near Dug-Out.

Through the medium of a letter written by an officer, Mrs. Ditchfield, 13, Dutton-street, has been informed of the death in action of her

husband, Private Harry Ditchfield, of the Loyal North Lancashire Regireent. The letter, written Under date .May 20th, stated that "he was killed yesterday afternoon. by a heavy shell, whilst sitting in front of a dug-out in the re- serve line. Ditchfield is a great loss to us, as he was one of our strongest fellows and hardest workers, and we—the officers and men of his company—fully share your sorrow." This letter was Only received on Wednesday, and the Monday before Mrs. Ditchfield received

reported to have died of wounds, and later wounded and missing, is now officially 'reported to have been killed in action on June 16th, 1915. This news has reached his parents at 5, Mill-. street, who for months have been anxiously awaiting news. Private Gibson, who worked as a plumber and painter With his father, received his education at the People's College, Arpley-street; and he formerly belonged to the choir of St. Paul's Church. have only got one finger to write with. I got it pretty hot and I am sorry to say I have lost my left eye, for I got a piece of shrapnel right into it\ Still, it is better to lose that than get killed. I also got hit in the left shoulder, both hands, and left. side of the head, so I reckon I am lucky not to have been killed outright. My left shoulder is all right now, and my right hand is nearly better, so I am doing fine. I got up to-day for the first time. Cheer up! the war is bound to come to an end some time. We are having lovely weather here." Rifleman Dickenson, who is 19 years of age, is one of two soldier-brothers, George being now in training. .Rifleman Dickenson worked at Orford Tannery before enlisting, and was edn- cated at the school .at Orford, in which district the family had livel almost all their lives, only leaving it very recently.

PRIVATE J. RA YNOlt

Bespattered With Shrapnel, But Li% ely For All That. Private John Raynor, whose wife lives at 83, Chorley-street, Warrington, has been wounded, but writes home a cheery letter, as will be seen

PRIVATE JOHN RAYNOR.

Wounded.

by the extracts we reproduce. Private Raynor enlisted in the Manchesters in the Second month of the war and went out to France last November. He attended. King- street,School as a boy, and was in the choir at St. Mary's Church, Butterniarketstreet. Some time ago he was in the employ of the SL Helens Cable Co. and played football for that company's works' team. At the time he en- listed he was in the Counting House of the Calico Printers' Association, Manchester. He, is 27 years of age. Private Raynor, writing under date May 19th, says :—" I am pleased to itfohn you that I am going on 'O.K.' -To-morrow I am getting up; having only been in bed for a ,week. On the evening of the 11th, just at dusk, seven of us were going to take up our positions on what is called an advanced post. We had just got about half-ay there when 'Fritz' started sending over shrapnel. Anyway, we Continued to go 'along. until he put two shells in quick succession quite close to us. Just as we were making another . start, One bursted right amongst the seven of us. At first I thought the moon had dropped and hit me. I was abso- lutely covered with dirt, and everyone .of us, with the exception of one chap, had been hit, afid he was suffering terribly from shell shock. I can congratulate myself on my lucky escapes. In the first place, I must say that my shrapnel helmet saved My life. One piece went straight through it, but apart from a small scalp wound no further ,damage was done in that quarter. Another piece caught me in the stomach—this is the piece which put me in bed. Another fraction of an inch with this wound and I would have 'gone West' all right. Giving it you as the doctor put i, 'I am the luckiest/ man in the world.' Of the four other pieces that "found their way through my great-coat, one went through the cuff of my left sleeve, another piece through the right sleeve near the elbow, while the remaining two pieces also went through my coat. There was a hole in my shrapnel helmet as big as a five-shilling piece, so you can form an idea of how much larger the bole in my 'nut' would have been lint for the. helmet. I should not have been writing you now; I should have become a land- owner a week ago."

SERGEANT H. HEATH.

Writes That He Is Progressing Favourably.

Sergeant H. Heath, of the North Staffs Regi- ment, whose home is at Burton-on-Trent, but whose wife now resides with relations at 38, Algernon-street, has been wounded in action, but, according to the' latest news received, ;s progressing favourably. He is an old soldier, and has been at the Front some time.

THE THEATRES.

Royal Court.

" Bella Donna" is a notable play notably acted by Mr. Dudley Stuart and Mr. Gerald Alexan- der's London Company.

Particularly in the principals have we been fortunate. Miss Georgina Wynter has been a wonderful success.

Mass Wynter is a lady or classical contour. As the, daughter of a c/ergyman, she met Canon Willis. on several occasions at her lather's house at. Wellingborough, and, she added with a smile, "What he admired most about me was my hair."--soft brown, abundant and billowy. She is the most beautiful lady who has acted at the; Royali Court this year. Miss' Parry, who was with Alr. Norris, also stands out in tho memory,. but she, *of another type.. _

When "Bella Donna" was here about three years ago Miss Wynter was playing :Mrs: Chep- stow,.but she has not continued thus ever since. She 'remained Mrs. Chepstow for a year, and. then helped' topopularise "A Pair of Silk Stock- ings" in the provinces, afterwards going into. "Within the Law," as the elusive shop girl.

Here was a part she esteemed even wore than that of Mrs. Chepstow, for it gave her ample opportunity to display her ability in strong' emotional characterisation. One gets rich Beams- of it in her meetings with Baroudi.

Miss Wynter, 1" may say, is the wife of Mr. Dudley Stuart, the Hon. Nigel Armine.

Mr. Frank Henry, who has created a very good' impression as. Dr. Meyer rsaacson and seems to have made a lif study of the character, has, as a matter of fact, only been with the company for three months. Until three or four months ago he was a leading baritone in Robert Courtneidge's productions. HIS wife is Miss Julie Venard (Mrs. Marchmont) who, until she attached her- si4f to this company, played" Lady Isabelle fn "The Whip."

Mr. F. Marriott-Watson's company will occupy the boards at the Royal Court Theatre next week, when- "What Happened at Mid- night" will be presented twice nightly. Mr. and Mrs. Marriott-Watson will play the lead- ing Parts in the di-ama.

A WONDERFUL WOMAN COMING TO WARRINGTON.

La Somna At The Hippodrome. At the Hippodrome, Warrington, next week, there is appearing the most wonderful woman in the world.' In private life she is a delightful little person. In. the modern prophetess one would expect to find, some fabled ogress, or some • spiritualised mystic. I found neither (writes a representative Who interviewed her), but a dainty little lady, who sat playing with her Porn, and spoke with just the faintest suspicion of a lisp. And this was La Somna, the little lady who set all London talking, and who is equipped with the most wonderful gift. She has appeared several times before royalty and very large Society audiences. Even her little Porn, has its little tricks, and washed its tiny face for me, and with an exclamation of disgust told me its opinion of the Kaiser. It is an amazing and haunting performance which La Somna gives, and not the least striking is the easy man- ner in which she answers the thousands of ques- tions put to her. A question paper is handed' about the welfare of their future husbands at the Front. "The most trying part of my work," she said, "is answering hundreds of the many thousands of letters I receive from the patrons of the halls which I vAit: Just as it is impossible for me to reply to all the questions put to me, so is it impossible to answer all MI correspondents. Sometimes it takes half a day to open my letters, and I can only reply to the most urgent communications. I should like to reply to all, but were I to attempt to do so, the nature of my work and the tremendous strain it puts upon me, would soon Make me a physical wreck. That is an imnortant point overlooked to you as you enter the theatre, on which is. printed, "Please have your question written.

LA SOMNA.

and ready for La Sbmna, to answer." No invita- tion could be more direct and plain, yet many people appear to be hypnotised by the demand, or overcome by trepidation as to the result; but there is nothing to fear, as the wonderful woman answers the most delicate inquiry with the greatest tact. Her answers are given with, the greatest rapidity and accuracy, and the following are some of the questions replied to at a recent exhibition of her powers:— " Where is my wife?"' "When shall I get married?" "Will my husband return safe?" vr• "What is my regimental number?" "Who stole my diamond ring?" "When shall I retire from business?" "Where is my brother?" "Will I receive a money. settlement?" "' What is my next dividend'?" "Is the ticket I hold a winning one?"' "Am I engaged. If so, who to?" "Where was I wounded?" • "Will I pass my examination ?"- " Who wrote an anonymous letter to me?" As La Somna remarked, a very large number of inquiries come from young women anxious by many, .and that is why I cannot make my performances too long. Some don't care what happens to me if they get their questions answered. On the other hand, lam deeply indebted for many kind letters received from people to whom I have been instrumental, by the powers given to me, in bringing a little brightness into their lives." • La Somna was born at Newton Abbot, Devon. "It was there," she remarked, "I was dis- covered. By accident it was found out that I could read the mind's of people around' me, and had located the whereabouts of missing friends, and how to recover lost and stolen property. My parents were approached to allow me to go on tour through the country." "How keg ago is that?" "I must not tell you my age," said La Scmna in a charming manner, and added with a laugh, "Since that time I have toured London and the provinces. At the London l'allodium I demonstrated my powers to nearly fifty thousand people in one week." La Somna has delighted, charmed, and amazed many brilliant Society audienc',s, including a distinguished company at the Vio4,regal lodge. Dublin, amongst those present bring Lord and Lady Aberdeen, Duke of Westminster, Earl and Countess of Carrick, Field-Marshal Sir John French, General Forestier Walker, and many other notabilities. Their Excellencies Lord and Lady' Aberdeen thanked La Somna personally for her visit, and remarked that such powers as she possessed were really marvellous and the most wonderful they had ever seen. "You know whet they say of the seventh daughter of a seventh daughter," said La Sonana, "that she is gifted with some special gift. Well, I am one of those daughters, and my powers are simply inherited. Why, I cannot tell, but it 'does not retrain with me for very long periods. 'Often I think it may be taken from nie at any Moment, this. Dower of concentrating my will .so as to meet other people's brain waves, for if anyone puts a question to me, he or she must , be thinking about it so that I read what is passing through the min'!" • -Trulr, there are ore.)4.iiinge .in heaven and !earth than rn are draggat giti o philtitiophy.l

===== 0002601_19160527_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
laters to the (Ettitrit THE LATE MR, T. NIXON.

IRISHMEN

Sir,—l was indeed surPrised when I read in your issue of Saturday, last. the remarks of one or two .Members of the Rural' District Tribunal in reference to. the troubles of-,a 'publican, who had to look after the Irishmen who came into his public-house. It_ is very painful to Irish- men to have continually to hear and to read such remarks. Because a few 'fanatics rise up in rebellion are Irishmen in this country to be libelled in this manner and to be told that they have no right to be here? I wonder if Mr. Ball would like to send back to Ireland those brave Irish lads who are fighting in Flanders, and in every other place where there is fighting to be done for the Empire? Is the time so long past that the gallant deeds of the Dublins, Mun- sters, Connaught Rangers; and also the Ulster Volunteers, who are now fighting side by side with their Nationalist countrymen-, are for- gotten? Can we not point with pride to the dash and bravery of the Irish troops in far away Gallipoli, where so many of them laid down their lives in the sacred cause of liberty? But still in spite of all that Ireland and Irishmen heve done, and are still doing, there are individuals who never miss a chance to cast a. slur on the fair name of Ireland and on the Irish race. This is not the way to get the best out of the Irish people, whose loyalty, by the way, is the admiration of the whole world. In conclusion, I would like to offer to the Irishmen concerned in this case a little advice. Keep clear of public-houses, be sober and tem. perate, and you will then be a credit to the land that is proud to own you as her sons, and, at the same time, you will allow the publicans who so desire to go and do their bit with an easy mind.

A HIBERNI..kN,

===== 0002601_19160527_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
GREAT BRITAIN AND GERMANY.

Sir,—l read very carefully and pleasurably the instructive and interesting article on "The Possible Economic Arrangements between Great Britain and Germany after the War," contributed to your columns a few weeks ago by Sir A. H. Crosfield. I think it is a subject deserving further extension, but one requiring a far abler pen than mine to deal with*. There: fore I„vielcomed the two vigorous and 'capable responses it has drawn from your correspond- ent, "Phil Squod," with whom I seem to feel in full agreement. But there is just one phase of the subject which presents itself to me, upon which I would like an expression of opinion, if possible, from both our friends, or others, who may be interested. It is this: Are we to be satisfied after the war with dealing with a Ger- many, constituted as she was before the war? I am one of those hoping and believing in the termination of the war, with Great Britain hav- ing a prominent voice, not in the mere arrange- ment, but in the dictation, of the terms of

peace. My impressions are that nearly all our public men, and the Press generally, agree, that Ger- many' entered into this war (for which she' will have to pay the price) through the dominating influence of Prussia. When Austria and Prus- sia declared war upon Denmark and defeated her, they denuded Denmark of Schleswig-Hol- itein—Austria being then head of the German confederation. Prussia soon after, having ap- propriated Schleswig-Holstein, declared war upon Austria, defeated her, deposed the King of Hanover, pushed Austria out of the German confederation, appropriating Hanover as part of the Prussian dominion, and assuming the headship of the confederation herself. Then was engineered the war with France, the result being the seizure of Alsace-Lorraine and the crowning of the King of Prussia as German ,Emperor. I have roughly outlined these events, as they create precedents, and we are very much influ- enced by precedents. Naturally I do not pre- sume to say what statesmen ought to do, but I have opinions and ideas, which if I could give effect to them would be something.as follows: The re-creation of the State of Poland, also of Hanover and Bohemia, the peoples of each to choose their own forms of government.- If a monarchy, their own ruler; Alsace-Lorraine re- turned to France; Prussia I would put back to her original boundaries, and outside the Ger- man States, refusing to recognise her as having any part or voice in their destinies. As I have shown, she has created these, precedents for herself. May I remind your readers that United Europe, and not France herself deposed Napoleon I. and exiled him as a menace to the peace of Europe? I regard the present great conflict, in which we have lost thousands of the finest specimens of British manhood, as the outcome of the assumption and arrogance of the -present Ger- man Emperor, his family and intimates (he has brooked no adverse advisers) and unless he and they are personally punished and humiliated, I, for one at least, will feel we have not fully and efficiently achieved the end for which these brave boys have died. 125, Wilderspool-road,

PATRICK O'BRIEN.

May 22nd, 1916,

AND PUBLIC-HOUSES.

Funeral of Well-known Licensee And Sportsman.

Ofi Monday a large number of relatives and friends attended the funeral of Mr. T. Nixon, who passed away at his residence, the Black Bear Inn, on Friday of last week. The deceased gentleman, Who was fifty-five years of age, had been in failing health for some time past, and since February had been confined to his room. He was a keen and prominent sportsman, being a leading official of the Warrington Anglers' Association, trea- surer of the Mersey Gun Club, president of the Latchford Garden Society, and treasurer of the Latchford Angling Ststiety. He was also a member of the Wine and Beer Sellers' Associa- tion, and chairman of the Rational Friendly Society( The interment took place at the Grappenhall Parish Church, being preceded by a short ser- vice conducted by the Rev. H. V. Pigot at the home of the deceased. The chief mourners were: Mr. John Nixon and Mr. Joseph Nixon (brothers), Mr. S2Snutch (half-brother), Mr. D. Patterson (son-in-law),

Substantial Increase Caused By ' Epidemic Disease.

The rate to be asked for at the ,meeting of the Lymm Urban District Council, on Monday night, will be 48. 4d. in the £ against 3s. 3d. last year. This increase of is. id. in tho £ has been caused by the recent epidemic of scarlet fever and diphtheria, which cost some £5OO or £6OO.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
Father Starts Business For His Sons.

A coal merchant applied for exemption for his son (35), stating that he was his parent's chief support, and that he was indispensable to the working of the business. He bought the business five years ago, and his two sons assiste.l to manage it. ,He bought it chiefly for his sons. Both sons were married men, and the son appealed for had three children. The other had been rejected as medically unfit by the military authorities.

Chairman: Could not you manage the business with yourself and the one son? Applicant: The son referred to recently had to be off work for 26 weeks, and I myself am 63 years of age.

Chairman: Are you healthy? Applicant: Pretty fair; and I have to do my fair share of work.

The Tribunal decided to grant eiemption un- til June 19th.

10" DISTRICT PIEW, -,:j

LYMM

LOCAL FATS AND FANCIES.

By CRITIC. People seem to have got used quite auto- matically to the new "summer time."

Why didn't the Church bells ring last Sun- day morning? They failed to herald the new "summer time."

Possibly the bellringers forgot to put their clocks an hour forward before retiring on Satur- day night.

Anyhow, the one bell that did ring, fairly " tolled ". the tale.

Oh! it's s'nice to get up in the morning, but it's s'nicer to stop in bed.—llarry Lauder.

What a time they had at Statham last Satur- day! And what a handsome contribution was raised fo; the shirt and sock fund!

What a profitable game whist really is-2-13 is. 6d. was made at the whist drive at Wormald Green last week!

Have you bought your flag yet? If not, why not?

Remember what you are paying your money for. Not for the flag itself, but to help to build workshops for our maimed and disabled soldie7..s and sailors.

Also a smile with every flag. Try it, boys!

What is wrong with our Council Offices? k gentleman told me the other dak that they would always be incomplete as long as they did not possess a public clock.

I politely told him that 'we were economising in "

I saw Private R. Smith, who is home from the, Front, the other day, and he certainly looked the picture of health.

I was very sorry to hear that nothing had yet been heard of his brother, Private Victor Smith, who has been missing several months.

The sale of flags at the Primrose League con- cert on Thursday night realised A:2 Os. 2d.

Mr. Arthur Schofield is fast making a name. During his monologue "The Sheep and the Goats" he fairly held the audience spell- bound. '

His talents do not stop at singing. He sings several of his own songs.

The duets ;rendered by Messrs. Poole and Rabbitt were another speciality. The sweet blending of their voices was a remarkable feature.

The members very rightly recognised the valuable services of their officers in the past and re-elected them for the coming year.

What do you think of this "The life of a soldier"? I received it from a friend of mine the other day:—

THE DAILY LIFE OF A SOLDIER. Written By Himself. 6.0 a.m.—Reveille--" Christians, Awake." 6.45 a.m.—Rouse Parade—" Art Thou Weary, Art Thou Languid?" 7.0 Parade—" When He Cometh." 8.15 a.m.—Manceuvres—" Fight the Good Fight." 9.30 a.m.—Company Orders—" Oft in Danger, Oft in Woe." 10.30 a.m.—Kit Inspection—" All Things Bright and Beautiful." 11.45 a.m.—Swedish Drill—" Here We Suffer Grief and Pain." 1.0 p.m.—Dinner—" Come All Ye Thankful." 2.15 p.m.—Ride Drill—" Go Labour On." 3.15 p.m.—Lecture by Officer—" Tell Me the Old, Old Story." 4.30 p.m.—Dismiss—" Praise God From Whom All Blessings Flow." 5.0 p.m.—Tea—" What Means This Anxious Eager Throng ?" , 6.0 p.m.—Free for the night—" 0 Lord Rosy Happy We Shall Be." 6.30 p.m.—Out of Bounds—" We Do Not Know, We Cannot Tell." 7.0 p.m.—Route March—" Onward, Christian • Soldiers." 9.45 p.m.—Zeppelin Raid Drill—" We Plough the Fields and Scatter." 10.0 p.m.—Last Post—" All Are Safely Gathered In." 10.30 p.m.—Lights Out—" Peace, Perfect Peace." 11.0 p.m.—Night Manceuvres—" T.he Day Thou Gayest Lord Is Ended." 11.30 p.m.—Guard Inspection—" Sleep On Be- loved."

J. W.

LATE PRIVATE JOHN STATHAM.

Last Letter To Ills Mother.

Last week we recorded the fact that Private John Statham, a Newfields soldier, had been killed in action. He was 37 years of age, and leaves a widow and three children. He enlisted in NoVember, 1914, and was drafted out to the Dardanelles in ,June, 1915, and was wounded in the head in August, and returned to duty in October of the same year. He was employed by Councillor J. H. Holt, builder, of Barrs Bank, and worshipped at the Congregational Church. At one time he was connected with the local football club. " That Will Be a Day—Won't It?"

Writing to his mother a few days before he was killed, Private Statham wrote:— " I am just snatching time to send a few, lines, hoping to find you both in good health, as it leaves me at present. We have just been moved again, but I am not allowed to tell you 'where, or anything at all about the place that we are at. I hope our Arthur comes through all right, and I hope that with God's help we shall very soon ..be together again. That will be a day, won't it? Please remember me to all at home. I cannot tell you. all I should like

11-

to in a letter, but you know the feeling that exists, so I hope that you will excuse this short letter. Good-night and God bless you both." This was possibly the last letter that the gallant soldier ever wrote, for a few days after- wards he was killed. It was received at Moston- street, Newfields, tour days after the official War Office communication, which told of his death.

The brother Arthur referred to is at present serving "somewhere in France."

===== 0002601_19160527_art0137.txt | included=true | included =====
A WORD ABOUT THE CAMERON HIGHLANDERS.

By PRIVATE ERNEST SHAW. [Continued.]

FEBRUARY, 1915.

What weather! Rain, hail and frost! Every- thing to make life as unpleasant as possible, and as we muffled ourselves up and wrapped ourselves tighter in our 'great-coats our thoughts would wander to the bright, cosy kitchen fires at home, and a smile would light up our faces as we glanced down the trench and saw the men ,clamouring round the small coke fire, all eagerly listening to the funny yarns of a typical old Scotsman. How did we get on with the Belgians? Well, very well. They rather liked the Scotsmen in their tartan kilts and coloured gaiters,' and very often inconvenienced them- selves to get in our company. We often won- dered if they were really in earnest when they sang to us their praises, and very often doubted them, until one day we happened to come across a Belgian's diary, which contained the following extract :

"They won our approval, the tall Englishmen with their beautiful uniforms, but I prefer the Scottish soldiers; really fascinating with their little caps with the floating ribbons, their tartan kilts, which swing rhythmically to the measure of their elegant march, their light- coloured gaiters fastened round their shapely legs, which contribute to give them that 'lordly' air of theirs., The Scots are the most splendid soldiers in the world. I would have liked to tell them so, but, unfortunately, I did not understand a word of English." Anyhow, let us get on with the diary. February Ist.—Two of our officers joined ranks of London Scottish. February 2nd.—Second-Lieutenant sent down to base, sick.

February 4th.—Captain Grieve and Lieuts. Wilson and Mclntyre joined us from England. Special training given to snipers and machine gun sections. February 7th.—A draft of 37 arrived from —' under Lieuts. Pattison and Sloan. February 15th.—Battalion moved to other billets in the village of , about one mile off. Arrival of fresh draft of 102.

February lith.—Men issued with flat High- land bonnet instead of the Glengarry cap. These bonnets looked very well, but appear to have been made for Light Infantry or Rifles, as they are dark. green, with dark green toories, no dicing. February 21st.—Lieut. Peto, M. 8., R.A.31.C., replaced Lieut. Milne, M.D., R.A.M.C., as medi- cal officer to the battalion.

February 25th.—General inspected the batta- lion,

• February 27th.—Marched from p.m. and went via , over canal. Here we took over breastworks, not trenches. Occu- pied firing line.

SCHOOL CHUMS MEET IN EGYPT.

Private James Jennings Longs To See

It Raiti.

Writing to his home at Moston-street, New- fields, Private James Jennings says:— "I have got the Paper with poor Sam's re- pqrt in, and I think it is very good, so I .have cut it out and mean to keep it. We are still in the same place, with nothing to see but sand, more sand, and still more sand. You ask me if I have had a change yet. I am glad to say I have. We are having almost every eomfo-rt that's needed at present—good food; and, what is , still a greater luxury, plenty of water to wash our clothes with. You say that you are going to send me some socks. Don't I wish I had them now, for I have only one pair, and those are the ones that are at present decorating my feet. We get a lot of marching to do, -so. YOU can imagine that one pair is not of much conse. quence. It would amuse you if you saw us on washing day, for we have to take our socks off to wash them, and go barefooted until they are dry. Of course, • it- only takes-a matter of •about 10 minutes Or a quarter of an hour to dry them.

Otherwise we are all right, for, as I said before, we have plenty of everything. "I believe you are having some very damp weather over in England, and I suppose that accounts for most of the sickness that is spread- ing about. You ought to see the diseases that we have to fight against. No wonder they inoculate us, 'for what with the dirty streets and bad sanitary arrangements, one could not be sur- prised at anything. It is quite a common sight to go out in the desert and come across dead camels lying in the full glare of the sun. The natives don't seem to care a rap. Oh! how I wish it would. rain! I don't think I have seen it rain out here yet. You can always depend on a nice day, only when its windy; then it's get under cover. If you don't—well, you get blinded with sand!

Would Have Been a Fine Easter.

"You say that Corporal Agthur Read has just been over on furlough. I wish that I could have been over too. Willie Taylor and I, would not we have had a time the three of 'us? You can depend on it that it would have been the finest Easter of my life. Well, I hope he had a good time, for he has done his bit, and well-earned his furlough. I was out on Good Friday with Willie Taylor, and a very good time we had together. We did intend to have our photo- graphs taken,'frat when we saw what they were going to cost, we reserved our judgment. Fancy 4s. for half a dozen, they must think we are made of money. Perhaps- it would surprise them if they knew that we were the proud possessors of five bob every fortnight." Private James Jennings is a well-known local lad, and the William Taylor referred to is an nlO ar.hr,nl chum whom he accidentally met with in Egypt.
