• FLAG DAY.

To-day's Great Effort For Soldiers' Workshop Fund. Yesterday was "Flag Day" in this district, and to-day will again be "Flag Day," when a great effort is to be made to raise money for the LoTil Roberts Memorial Workshop Fund. The workers responsible for supporting this worthy oL)ject locally are Chairman and Hon. Tr3a.3ur,3r.--The Rev. 0. B. Thurston. Hon. Secretary.—Mr. F. E. Evans. Committee.—Rev. C. Egerton-Warburton, Rev. Pi. B. Lovell. D. Burrows, Mr. J. G. Lister Farrar and Mr. W. T. Limb. The district is divided up into several wards, and each ward w 4 be in charge of a President, as follows: • Lymm Ward.—Mrs. T. J. Ridgway. Heatley Ward.—Mrs. Tudor Evans. Booth's ,Hill Ward.—Mrs. A. W. Callender.. Statham Ward.—Mrs. Slaney. Warburton Ward.—Mrs. Egerton-Warburton. Partington Ward.—Rev. J. Batey. Thelwall Ward.—Rev. C. M. Johnson. That Broken Soldiers May Be Independent. After former wars little has been done to provide for the soldiers who have been wounded. The Lord RolArts Memorial Work- shop Fund seeks to make this reproach impos- sible after the present war. It is proposed to build workshops in which our unfortunate heroes are to be trained in the best way that, 'they can make a living. What are these workshops? They. are light, airy factories, several of which were inaugurated at the close of the South African War, and have proved most valuable in turning helpless cripples into valuable citizens. They will serve a double purpose—they will provide useful, healthy occupation for the men employed, and also help . England to produce goods that in many cases have hitherto been provided by enemy countries. How can you help in this good work? By giving every assistance that lies in your power to this scheme. Let Lymm to-day see to its proud record of helping to the uttermost every good cause that stands in need of friendly and material assist- ance.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0141.txt | included=true | included =====
LANCE-CORPORAL E. HADDEN.

"Nearer Old Fritz," But Does Not See Much Change.

Writing to an employe in the Post Office, Lance-Corporal Ernest Hadden states "At last I am finding time to write you a few lines and I trust that they will find you well and hearty, as you usually are. Allow me to thank you very much for the socks you so kindly sent me; they are very comfortable. They have recently undergone a severe test with a long march, at the conclusion of which my feet were in splendid condition. Much depends on the socks you wear during these long marches. I suppose you often wonder how things are going on out here. Well, I don't think there is the slightest change from what it was this time last year. I think that by the time you receive this letter we shall be up the line again and a little bit nearer 'Old Fritz.' It will seem like a new thing to us, for it is fully four months now since we saw the trenches. What dp you think of the new Bill? I .am 'sure it will be very hard for some folks, hut the sooner we get this horrid business over the better it will be. Remember me to all at the Post Office, and tell them that I shall be pleased to receive a line any time." Lance-Corporal E. Hadden was mobilised at the outbreak of war with the Cheshire Regi- ment and was drafted out to France some 16 months ago. He is a single man and has several brothers serving with the colours. Prior to the war he was employed at the Post Office as a postman.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0142.txt | included=true | included =====
STATHANI'S LITTLE EFWORT.

Successful Effort For Sock And Shirt Funfl.

Lymm has encouraged its lads to go and do their bit, and right loyally they have responded. While they are away everything is being done, for their comfort. People at home have realised what it really means to be on active service, and the hardships they have to endure. Dirt and filth are the greatest enemies that our gal- lant " Tommies " have to fight. We at home mast supply him with what is necessary to wipe out these treacherous foes. To enable this to be done a shirt and sock fund has been organ- ised, and up to the present it has met with tre- mendous success.' On Saturday the people of Statham rose to the occasion, with the result that .210 has been handed over to Mrs. Thurston, the hon. treasurer. This was raised through the means of an entertainment and sale of work, which was organised by Miss Dalton, of Ivy Cottage, who was assisted by Mesdames Watson, Hench and Proud, and the 'Misses Guthrie, Dixon, Little, Walton, Wright and Dalton, and took place in the Statham Schools. The principal attractions were a hoop-la stall, which was exceedingly popular, especially amongst the ladies and wounded soldiers from Brookfield, who were the guests of the after- noon. A stall which displayed household and fancy goods was very, well patronised, and a jumble sale, conducted at the other end of the room, judging by the way the various bargains disappeared, was perhaps the most attractive of the Whole lot. During the afternoon an enter- tainment was given by several local ladies, and was thoroughly enjoyed by the whole company.

I The annual Meeting of the Lymm Habitation of thet Primrose League was held at the Con- servative Club on Thursday evening, followed by a social and concert. A Pretty Khaki Wedding.—Oughtrington Church was on Wednesday the scene of a very pretty khaki wedding, the contractinc, parties being Sapper James Wood, of the Engineers, eldest son of Mr. Wm. Wood, builder, Broom Edge, Lymm, and Margaret nightield, youngest daughter of Mr. and Mrs. Geo. Highfield, Tower Cottages, Lymm. The officiating clergyman was the Rev. G. S. Tudor-Evans. The bride, who was charmingly attired in a dress of soft material of a lovely shade of blue, and carried a sheaf of lilies, was accompanied by four bridesmaids, Miss Ada Wood and Miss Nellie Clay, and two little girls, Peggie and Maggie Wood, who carried baskets of pink tulips and smilax. She was given away by her father. The best man was Mr. James Young, of War- rington. Sapper Wood, who enlisted in Septem- ber, 1914, is about to go to the East on active service. He has two brothers and the bride one brother already on active service.

STOCKTON HEATH.

EMPIRE DAY.

The Empire Of School And Of Self.

At the Church of England School, Stockton Heath, on Wednesday, the scholars were ad- dressed in the yard by Mr. Rose, the head- master, who spoke of the Empire, the empire of school and the empire of self. Dealing with the British Empire, he referred to its great size and countless productions and pointed out that an empire consists of people; that 225 pieces, i.e., citizens of the empire, were now assembled; and that good citizens make a good empire, bad citizens make a bad empire. At this point the National Anthem was sung. _ _

Mr. Rose, dealing with the empire of school, said the school consists of scholars; if scholars are good, so is school; bad scholars make bad school.

The school song (composed by Mr. Rose) was sung. The chorus runs:—

"Then scholars all, great and small, do your best, Let honour be your rule. You must work and play, ever fight and pray, For the honour of the dear old school."

The empire of self, said the Headmaster, is the most important empire—good selves make good school and a good empire—the higher self must rule.

A verse of "0 God, our help in ages past," was sung, and the .flag was unfurled. The scholars did not cheer, but were told that when they saw a Union Jack it must remind them of the Britisli Empire, the empire of school, and the empire of self—the last empire being always the most important.

STRAIN.

Foot misery is 'often the first diffieulty encoun- tered by munition workers who have to remain on their feet all day. I found, however, that all such tortures can be instantly relieved and permanently cured by simply resting the feet a few minutes in a warm foot-bath containing a tablespoonful of ordinary Reudel,Bath Saltrates. This softens even the hardest corns so they come right out at the touch, root and all. The feet being the farthest points to which the blood must be pumped, foot troubles are usually due to defective circulation and irritation in. these extremities. The hot saltrated bath will stimu- late the circulation, instantly soothe and relieve any painful congestion due to shoe pressure, reduce swelling and open the clogged pores. This renders the skin active and healthy, pre- vents offensive perspiration odours, and banishes smarting, burning, itching or other foot afflic- tions for all time. It is marvellously effective for soldiers' "trench foot," chilblains, bunions, and similar troubles, even including rheuma- tism, gout, &c. Most chemists keep the common refined Reudel Bath Saftrates ready put up in convenient packets, one of which should prove sufficient to permanently end all foot misery at slight cost. ,Try this refreshing foot-bath after coming in footsore from a long walk or after being on your feet all day. You will soon feel like dancing with joy, and your newest, tightest boots seem like the oldest pair you have.—D.L.C. IMPORTANT NOTE.—Upon enquiry we find that although supplies of the above compound are limited, local chemists can still supply reasonable quantities from stock, and there has as yet been no advance in price. Air in the case of all drugs, how- ever, we are told that a sharp rise may be expected shortly, and it is therefore advisable to obtain a supply while it is still to be had easily and at very low cost.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0149.txt | included=true | included =====
PADGATE.

Egg Collection.—The scholars of the Padgate C.E. Schools again gave evidence of their patriotism when a collection of eggs for the wounded soldiers was made in the schools on Thursday and Friday last week. The total number collected was 325, besides Gs. in cash. The eggs were distributed on Saturday to local military hospitals in the following proportions: Winwick, 100; Whitecross, 40; Raddon Court, 70; Thelwall, 70; Warrington Barracks, 45.

Church Parade.—On Sunday evening a church parade organised by the St. Elphin's, St. Paul's and Padgate Companies of the Church Lads' Brigade took place. Headed by the various bugle bands the lads attended the service held in Christ Church, Padgate. The Rev. J. Hirst preathed from the Brigade motto, "Fight the good fight," and the National Anthem was heartily sung at the conclusion of the service. After service a combined route march was ar- ranged, under the command of Major Brooke, the route being along Fearnhead-lane to Cinnamon Brow and back again. Captain Simpson was in charge of the Padgate Company.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0150.txt | included=true | included =====
PENKETH.

A Productive Jumble Sale.—Despite the heavy calls on both time and purse of all at the pres- ent time, the lady workers of the St. Mary's Mission at Penketh managed to clear over £25 at the jumble sale held in the Mission Room last week-end, which sum has been handed over to the church fund.

Empire Day.—On Wednesday a good number of parents and friends gathered to watch the Empire Day celebration at the Council School, Penketh. Dr. Murray was the chairman, and speeches were given by him and by the Head Master, Mr. Griffiths. The programme, which included patriotic songs, Maypole dances by the infants, and country dances by some of the older scholars, concluded with the National Anthem, after which the whole school, class by class, marched past and saluted the flag. Special mention was made in the speeches of the help which the children were able to give to the wounded soldiers in the local hospitals.

WHITLEY.

CONCERT PROCEEDS FOR SOLDIERS' COMFORTS.

• A successful concert was held on Tuesday in the Assembly Rooms, Grimsditch, to provide funds for the Whitley Working Party, who are busily engaged in making comforts for the soldier boys who have gone from the district. Mr. Cyril Clayton acted as accompanist, and to Mr. J. Clayton, of Thelwall, credit is due for organising the concert.

WOOD PULP FROM CANADA.

To Make Us Independent Of

Scandinavia.

Reuter's Agency understands ,that ,consider- able Progress has been made with arrangements for the establishment in Canada of a mill for .the production of sulphite pulp on a large scale, and it is expected it will be neeessary in future to import very little wood pulp to this country from Sweden. This will have the additional, advantage of favourably affecting the present rate of exchange. It has been stated in the 'Press that .the present cessation of imports of wood' pulp from Sweden was partly due to the action of Great Britain. This is not the case. The prohibition was one of export, and originated with the Swedish Government. The Swedish prohibi- tion will, however, for reasons mentioned above, turn out to be a blessing for Canada, and manu- facturers in this country are confidently hoping that the freedom from dependence upon Swedish supplies will be permanent.

WOMEN WAR WORKERS.

, During the. last twenty months or so of war things li'avp Changed so completely for everyone that E. seems as if. a, new world has come into being and that old modes of life were. no. longer possible: During these crowded months differ- ent things have appealed to different women as being of permanent importance, and the June issue .of "The Woman at Home • and Girls' Realm" gives some viqws of the leading women war-workers, including the Marchioness of Londonderry, Miss Swift, matron of the British Red Cross Society and the Order of Bt. John; Mrs. Henry Fawcett, LL.D.; Miss A. 13: F. Horniman, the witty and genial-lady who con- trols • the Gaiety Theatre, Manchester; and several others • who have .been alive to these Changes. Fiction in this issue is powerfully represented, and includes a long complete romance in the hest style of R. S. Warren Bell, and some furthes splendid stories by. Gertrude Aram .Annie S. Swan, J. E.' Buckrose, Cal Ewald, and others. Among the items of general interest to womenfolk the "Woman at Home and Girls' Realm" deals adequately with seasonable tmillinery, emphasising " Chic " beaded hats, home .dressmaking, and home cookery.

RUGBY NEXT SEASON P

War rgtc Club Conzia'eling P!.o3pect3.

LAST YEAR'S LOSS OF £324 EXPLAINED.

Theugli in a m'Aified way, there is every prospect of fouC;aii being'played in the Wilders- pool enclosure next season. Last year the Warrington Rugby Football Club sustained a loss of 5:321 12s. 2d. These facts were broached at the annual meeting of the club, held in the Pavilion on Monday evening. The place of meeting easily accommodated those who put in an appearance. Those present heartily received the Chairman's (Mr. E. Ohorne) announcement of the possi- bility. of Rugby being played. They were the lindens Of 'enthusiasm.: , _

Mr. T. Pemberton Submitted the accounts, Which showed' that while the receipts had been £l2B 13s. 10d., the 'contra account showed £453 ss. These included £SO paid .to the' officials for the season 1914-15, £63 interest on— mortgage, £BO ground expenses, £7O rents, rates. and taxes, and £llB depreciation. £324 seems a considerable ?Mount to lose during a year of suspension of football activity, but we must take into con- sideration the fiet that the honoraria granted at the annual meeting last May were for ser- vices rcndered in the season 1914-15, and we Must not 'forget that the depreciation :is rather extensive. The enormity of the figures are in that way easily understood. The worth of the land, buildings, S:c.,`: is £3,118, and there is a balance of .assetS over liabilities of £1,470.

I Considering all things, said the Chair Man, they 'could congratulate therriSelVes that the' loss was small, for the standing charges on a ground ,such, as their's were very Considerable. He thought they might say they had done fairly well: Of course, the one thing they were particularly keen about a this time was to save - and conserve as much as they possibly could, so that v•-lier. they came back, to the lime When they could have first-class. football, they would have sufficient cash to resume under sothewhat .abnormal conditions. That was one reason why members of the club ,should rally round and send in their subscriptions. In the 1914-15 season there were 116 honorary 'members, and out of these' only 59 had paid their sub- scriptions at the reduced.figure of 7s. 6d., which was half of the previous one. Whereas in 1915 they had 772 ordinary members, only 325 had paid the redueed subscriptions of 3s. Of course, a good many had joined the forces, and they were doing far better work elsewhere, but those who were-left should come forward, aS a matter of honour, and Pay the 'reduced suly.,.criptions,l at any rate., Regarding the, financial state- ment, he added_that when they compared it to the previous year, \ they had nothing to be ashamed of. They had cut down ex.nenses as low as possible. Next' year, if they had little football, they would be able to save on the ground, for they would not wind so much money. The treasurer had. presented a very_ good statement, and the committee had all done their work admirably. - The financial report Was adopted.. , -

===== 0002601_19160527_art0173.txt | included=true | included =====
NEXT SEASON'S PROSPECTS.

• Regarding the resumption of football, the Chairman said that that was on the "knees of the gods." , They had to be guided • very largely by what took place in the next few months, kind what might take place at, the de- ferred annual meeting of the Northern Union, to be held on the Ist August. It was considered that by that time all the clubs would have an idea of the 'advisability or not of playing a modified form of football. As far as Warring- ton was concerned, he reiterated what he said at the last annual meeting, that during war time, at any rate, they should not be a party' to pro- fessional ;football. (Applause.) At the same time, they were, not going to place themselves in the position of the "(log in the manger." They wanted to do all they could to help forward the game, and keep, it alive, and .if some ukodified scheme were put forward, they, would be only too. pleased to 'fall into line ,and. do what they Could. By Au4g,ust Ist they .Might have somAdea of what -1110 Y. were prepared to 'do. The 'diffi- culty,: of course, was in 'regard to players:., It, haa been felt very largely in the Intermediate. League. and Several clubs had had to fall out, because they could not find players. .Yet those. who, had. remained had 'produced .a 'ferin Of fOOt ball which :had been the means of keeping to- gether a number of good players who would be a great help 'in maintaining football at a high standard later bn. He (Mit Osborne) had recently seen. Mr. J.• H. Smith, who told' him that ke had 'been particularly struck by the way the Warringthn 'and Widnes' Intermediate Clubs had played, and .dded that Warrington should try to emulate Widnes and do all they could to get local players.. He (Mr. Osborne) was in hearty agreement with that sentiment. (Hear, hear.) • Foothaii No Hinr!ra.lce to Recruiting.

With regard to playing football next season, Mr. Osborne said that the conditions were not the‘sarne as last year, for by then compulsion would .be in force and all those who were not in the Army • would %. either be engaged on nrunition work or otherwise exempted from ser- vice, and, therefore, if they did resume foot- ball it could not be said by anyone or felt by themselves that they were hindering recruiting; in fact, be thought it would be. in the interest of the workers if some attractive football were played on Saturdair afternoons, for then men would be able to return to their Work with renewed energy and vigour.

In prosecuting a charge against three young workmen for absenting themselves from work, a works manager at a controlled establishment, told. the Warrington Munitions Court .on Mon- day that it had been reported to him that the men had spent most of the time in a public- house • watching a • billiards match. " It was alleged against the men ,that on the night of the 15th that they stayed', away from work, causing a loss of 45 10s. td the othq members of their squad, and about £l2 to the company The men went to supper and were an hour late in resuming work, causing a stoppage of one of ,the /mills. Having waited for the defendants sometime, the other men of the squad gradually left Work. • The defendants were aware that the work could not be proceeded with without them.

Defendants stated that there were not sufficient men present when they returned to carry•on the mill. _

Mr. T. J. Ridgway (the chairman) said the ,Court was satisfied that it was a serious case: If the defendants did not consider the compolly they, should have..cons.idercd; the iiy• tefests ,of their mates. Defendants Were 'fined' ssl, and the Chairman added • that understood that if another ease similar to their 3 carne before the Court a heavier fine would be imposed. Mr. W. P. 'Rylands and Mr. Frank Berry acted as assessors for the employers and the men respectively.

SHOOTING.

importanl Match For Trophy

At Appieton.

The Appleton Rifle Range on Saturday after- ndon was the venue of an important shooting competition between the 4th Battalion Manches- ter, Volunteer Regiment, and. the lstlßattalion •(GUard).. Regiment, Liverpool, •in • connection with 'the.- County Palatine of Lancaster Rifle a (Small Bore) Association for • the trophy, a handsome silver bowl, presented by Colonel J. Johnston, V.D., D.L., who is the regimental ir mmandant of the Lanca- shire Volunteer Brigade. There was a large gathering or interested 'spectators. The competition proper took place early in this. month, on • the competing clubs' own ranges, 41 teams entering. ;- The Manchester and Liverpool teams, cane out on top, and Appleton ;Range- was selected, for ,the final, The Men- ehester .team won by 28 points, the .seore,,•.b.ing Manchester 1.160, Liverpool. 1432. The, pf;s sible" was 1,200. _ _ _

Colonel Johnston, in presenting the trophy to the captain of the winning- team (Mr. A. 11. Parker), and silver spoons, .as a souvenir,• to the six members of each team, expressed the Rime .that' n great Many more .men -.lt-Cglit be induced to take up rifle shooting, not only be- Cause they would be rendering a national Kr- vice by doing so, but because it would be a pleasure to them. A nation of marksmen would tend to bring to a speedier conclusion the terrible struggle in Which we were now engaged.' Every marksman should realise fully the great responsibility that was placed on his.shouldars in utilising the knowledge he had gained at the hands of the musketry instructor. (Hear. hear.) The company later met at- the Lion Hotel, Warrington, , and had tea. Congratulatory speeches :were delivered, and the evening spent in harmony.

THE LAST OF THE GRACES.

Dr. Alfred .Grace, elder brother of the late pr. W. G Grace, died at his 'home at Chipping Sodbury, near Bristol, on Wednesday aged 76. He was the last survivor of the notable family of. crieketers,"*and was himself a good player, though never so prominent as his three brothers. He had been fifty years in medical practice at Chipping Sedbury.

MEMBER' FOR WIDNES.

Unopposed Re-election Of Colonel Hall Walker.

Nominations for the , Parliamentary vacancy in the Widnes Division closed on Monday. Colonel Hall Walker (Conservative), who had held the seat for sixteen .yeaA, was returned unopposed. He had the -support of Conserva- tives and Liberals. The by-election wag' neces- sitated by Colonel Hall Walker's retirement on his gift to the Government of his .racing studs .at Tulley and Riissley -Park, valued - at .V 75,000 and the sale ,on the Government's own valua- tion of both estates. • •

THE EXAMINERS SATURDAY, MAY 27, 1916.

THE TRIBUNALS.

MARRIED MEN APPEAL FOR EXEMPTION.

Business And Domestic Reasons Receive Considerate Treatment.

The Borough Tribunal on Tuesday morning was engaged for three hours in hearing appeals by married men wanting exemption on business and domestic grounds. Dr. G. W. Joseph pre- sided. Two appeals were heard in private, and the result was not announced. The decision in two appeals was adjourned, and in the re- mainder conditional and temporary exemption was given.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0195.txt | included=true | included =====
NOT FIT FOR A WOMAN.

A married man, aged 36, with three children, who carries on business as a hardware dealer and general furnishing business, appealed. He said his business entaiTed a good deal of labour, as he had to go out and buy second-hand furni- ture and do it up to make it saleable. His wife assisted him with the business, but he did not think the removing of furniture was a fit job for a woman.—He was given exemption till August 31st, and was asked to join the Volunteer Corps.

PUBLICAN'S TEMPORARY EXEMPTION. A publican, aged 32, married, with two chil- dren, appealed on business grounds. The Chairman pointed out that the hours of public-houses were shorter now, but appellant said although this was the case, he had to get through the business in the best way possible.-- Granted exemption till August 31st. Mr. Bradbury said such a healthy athlete-like appellant should join the Volunteer Corps. Appellant said he would attach himself to the Corps.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0196.txt | included=true | included =====
ONLY 4FT. 11IN.

Messrs. Ackers, Whitley and Co., coal mer- chants, appealed for A carter, married, aged 36, with four children. The . firm stated that the man was engaged as a bagger, in addition to which he had a coal round, which he was main- taining for a man who had joined the National Reserves, and who had fought in the Boer War. They did not think the man was fit for Army service; he was only 4ft. llin. with his clogs on. The application was adjourned to enable thc.! man to be medically examined.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0197.txt | included=true | included =====
BUILDING SOCIETY SECRETARY EXEMPTED.

An appeal on business grounds was made by an auctioneer, estate agent and valuer. The business was started 13 years ago. He took con- trol three years ago, on the death of the former principal. He had only one man in his employ; at the Commencement of the war his staff con- sisted of five men of military age. The bulk of his business was held on personal grounds, and would not be retained, if he had to join the Army. He acted as secretary to a local build- ing society and a limited company.—Conditional exemption was given.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0198.txt | included=true | included =====
SUPPLIED WORKPEOPLE'S DINNERS.

A tripe, fish and chip potato dealer, married, (35), with. two children, was given exemption till August 31st. He said he supplied a large number of dinners to workpeople.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0199.txt | included=true | included =====
"BEST APPEAL YET."

A cinema hall manager, who had put in an aPpeal, now expressed a desire to withdraw it. He said he had joined the Navy as•a signaller, and an undertaking had beeu given that he would not be called up till July 18th. He held an Army certificate for signalling. Dr. Joseph That is the best appeal we have heard yet. We wish you success, and hope you will come back a Lord High Admiral, or some- thing of the sort. • The Tribunal agreed to the appeal being with- drawn. •

===== 0002601_19160527_art0200.txt | included=true | included =====
INQUIRIES TO BE MADE.

Messrs. W. Almond and Co., tripe dressers, appealed on behalf of a works manager, aged 36, married, and two married oil refiners, aged 33 and 38 respectively In reply to Captain Mathams, the repre- sentative said there was no possible means of men being supplied from the headquarters at Blackburn. They were short-handed at Black- burn, and boys were chiefly employed. it would be prohibitive to send perishable goods from that town to Warrington. The Tribunal adjourned the appeals for a fort- night to enable inquiries to be made as to the conditions prevailing at Blackburn.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0201.txt | included=true | included =====
BOOTS FOR SOLDIERS' FAMILIES.

A master hoot and shoe repairer, aged 30, married, with two children, said his only assistant was his father, aged 60, who could not do a great deal of work owing' to infirmity. Most of his work was for soldiers' families. The Clerk: how do you know that? Appellant: Well, how do I know it? Most of the men our way are soldiers.

The Clerk: They don't stamp it on the soles of their boOts, do they? .(Laughter.l Exemption till the end of August was given.

WAR-TIME FRUIT SHOP.

fruit and vegetPble dealer was given till August 31st. He said he had two shops, one of which he had had eighteen months.

ThP Clerk: Wasn't it a risk to open a shop in war-time? Appellant said his father-in-law was looking after that shop.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0202.txt | included=true | included =====
TRIBUNAL'S FINAL.

An insurance agent (35), married, with four children, said he had tried to get someone to take over his book, but had failed. He asked for three months' exemption to enable him to look round. He was given till July 31st, and was informed that the decision of the Tribunal was final.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0203.txt | included=true | included =====
POSTING MANAGER EXEMPTED.

Mr. P. Maginn, cab proprietor, appealed for his general manager-clerk, aged 36, married, with two children. Mr. Maginn said he was also contractor for his Majesty's mails. He had failed to get suitable men to drive.— Exemption till the end of July was given,

===== 0002601_19160527_art0204.txt | included=true | included =====
LADY WINDOW CLEANERS.

Will They Be A Success in Warrington? When a window cleaner and carpet beater applied for exemption before the Tribunal at the, Town Hall on Thursday, he was asked whether ladies were not proving good substi- tutes for men. • Applicant: From my experience, I am afraid they won't be much of a success. The Clerk (Mr. A. T. Hallaway): Some of them were in trousers the other day and looked very nice. They were cleaning the windows of the Town Hall. Applicant: I don't think it is the sort of work for a woman. Exempted until 30th June.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0205.txt | included=true | included =====
BUSY COFFIN-MAKERS.

In applying for a son, a coffin-maker men- tioned that it was work which men were loth to do, as it was not pleasant. In the last seven- teen months they had Made 570 coffins. The case was adjourned for a week, when the appeal of another son will be heard. Mr. T. S. Steel appeared for the applicant.

HELPS THE BOARD OF AGRICULTURE.

Mr. John Mainwaring applied for his manag- ing clerk, remarking flat besides having an ex- tensive business as an auctioneer and valuer, he held certain .appointments under the Board of Agriculture. One of the duties Was to send a monthly return regarding the crops in an area including over 200 townships. Some of this expert work was done by the clerk. Mr. Main-

waring also spoke of the depletion his staff had undergone. His managing clerk was absolutely essential to the business. Conditional exemption.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0206.txt | included=true | included =====
FOR DUBLIN BUT NOT BERLIN.

The Wigan Coal and Iron Co., Ltd. appealed for their Warrington agent and wharf manager, 34 years of age and married. In reply to the Chairman, the agent said he had only been passed for home service because of his eyes. When he was at Chester Castle they said to him that he was not fit for Berlin, but only for Dublin.

The Clerk: Is that what they marked on your .paper? (Laughter.) The Deputy-Mayor: You could see a Sein Feiner, but not a German. (Renewed laughter.) Conditional exemption'.

RYLANDS'S STAFF GREATLY DEPLETgD.

•-- - - - Mr. T. Leslie, secretary of Messrs. Rylands Bros., appealed for five clerks. Prior to the war, lie said, they had an officelstaff of fifty, and of these 38 were of military age. They had lost 18 through enlisting, and had been obliged to fill their places as far as possible with females. The manufactures they were engaged upon had considerably increased their work, thus necessi- tating the retention of a number of experienced workers who had not been " starred " as indis- pensable. With one exception, and he was medically unfit, all the five had agreed to join the Volunteer Training Corps. Conditional exemption was granted to three of the clerks, these being married, and the other two were exempted until the end of Jdne.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0207.txt | included=true | included =====
MANAGER FOR HIS FATHER.

Mr. Andrew Rathbone, iron and steel mer- chant, gained conditional exemption for his son, who manages his business.

PROVISIONS.

A provision dealer, with a business of his own in Market-place, was granted conditional exemp- tion. He is married, but has no children.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0208.txt | included=true | included =====
MOSTLY BUSINESS.

Finding Out The indispensables

In Local Firms.

Dr. Joseph presided at yesterday's sitting of the Borough Tribunal, when a number of appeals, chiefly on business grounds, were re- * viewed.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0209.txt | included=true | included =====
ONE LEFT OUT OF THREE.

Mr. Porshaw appeared in support of appeals put in by two brothers, both married, who were partners in a wholesale and retail fish business. Mr. Forshaw said another brother, also a part- ner, had joined the Army. One brother, who had no family had now agreed to withdraw his appeal and join. The Tribunal granted conditional exemption to the remaining brother. ,

===== 0002601_19160527_art0210.txt | included=true | included =====
TEMPORARY EXEMPTION.

A motor cycle and cycle agent and repairer, aged 30, married, with three children, appealed. He was represented by Mr. Steel. He purchased the business, twelve months ago. Exempted, until August 31st.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0211.txt | included=true | included =====
A KINEMA APPEAL.

Mr. E. Connor, proprietor of' the Star 'Cinema, appealed on behalf of an engineman-electrician. Mrs. Lightburn informed the Tribunal that the manager was called up at the outbreak of war. Three other employes had joined, and another man was called up for Monday. At present there were fourteen employes, including one wounded soldier and two pensioners. The rest were females. Application had been made to a number of big firms for an operator, but they were unable to supply one. Exempted until August 31st.

GROCERS CONDITIONALLY EXEMPTED.

c,,,rs. J. Richardson and Sons, wholesale grocers, appealed on behalf of their foreman, a married man, • and he was given conditional exemption. A junior partner in the firm was given conditional exemption. The senior part- ner said they had 28 employes left, which in- cluded nine females. Only two men had been certified as fit for general service. Five had already joined and another was called up. Similar treatment was given a shoemaker and clogger, aged 32, married, with four children. Messrs. Houghton, wheelwrights and black- smiths, appealed for the only tradesman they had left. They had been advertising many months for workmen, but had failed to get replies.—Conditionally exempted.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0212.txt | included=true | included =====
R.S:P.C.A. CLAIM FOR INSPECTOR.

Mr. Steel, representing the Royal Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to. Animals, appealed on behalf of the local inspector. Mr. Steel explained that the society had lost 120 out of 220 inspectors, and those who re- mained worked two districts. The society sug- gested to the Tribunal that the work done by their inspectors was of national importance. The Board of Agriculture had issued regulations for the preservation of live stock, which the inspectors of the society carried out. If more inspectors were taken away it would be impos- sible to replace them,' as they were trained in veterinary work. Mr. Steel added that the local inspector was not shirking his duty; the appeal had been put in by the society.—Conditionally exempted.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0213.txt | included=true | included =====
LOOMS STANDING.

Messrs. Silcock appealed on behalf of a fire- man, Mr. W. B. Forshaw representing the firm. It was stated that six men out of 25 had joined the colours. They had been unable to replace them. They had five looms standing out of ten, owing to having no men to work them.—Conditionally exempted.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0214.txt | included=true | included =====
INSURANCE AGENT GIVEN TIME

An insurance agent, aged 33, married, asketi for two months' exemption to enable him to train a man to look after his book.—The Tribunal agreed.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0215.txt | included=true | included =====
ACCOUNTANT'S APPEAL.

Mr. L. A. Voisey, chartered accountant, ap- pealed on behalf of a chartered accountant, aged 36, and married. Mr. Voisey said it would be impossible at the present time to fill the man's place. He had half-yearly audits coming

on, and it would be a great inconvenience if he were taken. He had three other employes of military age, but two of them had been described as medically unfit. He thought the man could bo relieved by the end of July.—The Tribunal agreed to exempt him until then.

"THE CASE OF CHARLES DUKES."

Question in House Of Commons In the House Of Commons on Wednesday, Mr. Snowden asked the President of the Local Gov- ernment Board if he could obtain a hearing before the Central Tribunal of the case of Charles Dukes, of Warrington, district organiser for the Gasworkers and General Labourers' Union, secretary of the Lancashire and Cheshire Federation of Trades Councils, secretary of the Warrington Trades Council, member of the War- rington Munitions Tribunal, member of the Advisory Committee, of the Labour Exchanges (Liverpool district), his appeals to the local and county tribunals having failed, and as hardship was likely to result to the workmen whose in- terests are in the hands of Mr. Dukes, whether he would have this case referred to the Central Tribunal in order' to have an authoritative deci- ismiopnorotnancae with the matterit whic Was likely to be of calling up for military ser- vice of married men.

Mr. Long said he was making inquiries into this case.

EX-PUBLIC OFFICIAL

Who Became Farm Manager

And Teamsman.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0217.txt | included=true | included =====
FATHER QUESTIONED.

Mr. W. Furness, the military representative, at Wednesday's sitting of the Warrington Dis- trict Tribunal, put some pointed questions to a Newton-le-Willows farmer, who appealed for his son, aged 27 years, described as a farm manager and teamsman, who, previous to January last was a public official. Mr. Furness said that last January appellant's son was desirous of joining the Army. Efforts were made to get him a commission, without success.

The appellant agreed that when his son wanted to join the Army he was a deputy. registrar.

Furness said a good deal of comment had been occasioned in the district respecting appellant's son. He wanted to know the reason •for the man leaving his position as deputy- registrar to go on the farm, and if the con- ditions on the farm had altered considerably since January last. The appellant said the conditions had changed considera:bly. 'When he knew that his head man was to be called up he said his son would have to come to manage the farm affairs. He had lost several men since January, and he now had only his son and another man. Mr. Furness: You had a farm bailiff ?—Yes.

Is it a fact that_he_was discharged?—No.

Appellant explained that his farm bailiff had been ill for six months. He was now back with him, but he was unfit for heavy work. Conditions had so altered, and he was so short- handed that if his son went away he (appellant) would have to give up the business he intended to do.

The Tribunal granted one month's exemp- tion, and appellant expressed regret that he would have to come before the Tribunal again. A Member: You will be able to •get plenty of Irishmen in a month's time.

Appellant: Irishmen cannot manage a farm. CATTLEMAN'S EXEMPTION UNTIL SEPTEMBER 30th.

A Padgate farmer, appealing on behalf of his cattleman, said he had been trying to get men for weeks. He had advertised frequently, but had not got one application. Part of the man's duties was to drive cattle into the town. Aged men and women could not do this work. Cattle took some managing in a busy town. Exempted till end of September.

VICTIM OF MURDEROUS ASSAULT. •

A Fearnhead farmer, appealing for his son, aged 22 years, said he had full control of the farm. He (appellant) was unable to take an active part in the management as he was the victim of a murderous assault some years ago, and two men were sent to penal servitude for it. He had two sons in the Army and another likely to be called up. Exemption till end of September was given.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0218.txt | included=true | included =====
FARM EMPLOYES' EXEMPTIONS.

Several farm employes were given conditional and temporary exemption. In one case a farmer said he did darting for a District Council, and his son, for whom he appealed, assisted him. The Rev. A. M. Mitchell said a man who did carting for a District Council was not indis- pensable to the farm. Another member declared that carting for a' District Council was not profitable.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0219.txt | included=true | included =====
THE KINEMAS.

Picture House.

"His Real Character" and "A Desperate Leap" are the titles of two fine dramas which have been presented at the Picture House this week, the former during the first half and the latter during the second. Both were brimful of excitement.

Next week "Sing of a Great City" for th first half, and "The Beast" for the latter half will be the "star" pictures.

Star K i nema.

There is now showing at the Star Kinema Henry Arthur Jones's fine play, " Masquer- adors." In the first part of. next week " The Voice of the Fog "—a Lasky film—will be shown, and in the second half, "The Foundling," where Miss Mary Pickford, said to be "the world's sweetheart," will shine.

Special coming attractions are: Pauline Frederick in "Bella Donna"; and Hall Caine's masterpiece, The Christian," with Derwent Hall Caine as Father Storm.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0220.txt | included=true | included =====
WI'CLIFFE P.S.A.

There was a good meeting on Sunday, pre- sided over by the Rev. T. T. James, who gave a welcome to Mr. J. H. J. Hampson, who gave the address upon "Fight the good fight." He said it was our supreme duty to fight for God, our country and our homes. He did not think and believe, tls some people did, that this was a war because of our national sins. Someone was to blame, and he laid that at the door of the Kaiser and his leaders. We were fighting for peace and righteousness, and should honour God by good lives, as well as being alert at all times for our country and looking after our homes. Mr. Yearsley and Mr. W. Beswick took part in the meeting. The President welcomed Mr. McConville, who was over on leave, being engaged in a transport section, and the Secretary referred to a message from. Mr. G. T. Yeates he had received.

===== 0002601_19160527_art0221.txt | included=true | included =====

Boys' Dangerous Experiment With

Gunpowder.

BARKER'S BIG BARGAINS.

Curiosity led to a rather serious accident 011 Monday evening., with the result that two thirteen-year-old boy,—Jack Seed, of Padgate- lane, and Arthur Podmore, 4, Steel-sfreet—rr cowed severe burns, which necessitated the!r removal to the infirmary. Seed's condition 15 not so serious, but Podmore is suffering from extensive burns to the hands, arms and faa- It appears that Seed, who is an employe of the Cheshire Lines Railway Company, is fond et experimenting. He invited Podmore to a be at his home, where they. were making use a e' quantity of gunpowder. Podmore was some wax, when either the match or the meltee; wax dropped on to the powder, causing al loua explosion, with the,above results. A neighbour applied oil to the burns and the boys Were immediately taken to the Infirmary, where they are progressing as well as can be expected-

Admission to Members only on production. of Membership Ticket. . Business Important. A goOd attendance requested. Subject to the Quarterly Meeting's approval, Dividend will .be paid on all purchases made during the Quarter ended 'April 18th at the rate of 3/- in the X. This means over £17,000 being returned to Members, saved to them by the Society in three months. The annual average Saving (returned in dividend) during the past four years exceeds £4 per member per annum. Prices are reasonable. Goods are of the Finest Quality. THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MAY 27, 1916.

of volunteers. In the event of an invasion the immediate danger would not be from the Kaiser's soldiers, but from the aliens who are ' masquerading as friends in our midst, who, , in the absence of a force to keep them in order, would secretly attack our railways and our works. Volunteers would be very valu- able as guards—perhaps more valuable than Regulars, as they know their own districts well. It is a small thing for those who escape from active service to decide to help in the work of defending their own homes. Better treatment is now being accorded the Volun- teers, and there should be a better response to the solicitations of those who bare calling for recruits.

WINS D.C.M. BEAMONT SCHOOL OLD COY • Jubilation Yesterday. . • Only 18 years of age, Private Joseph Heesom, of the Royal Welsh Fh4liers. ~‘,l of Mr. and Mrs. Joseph Heesom, 31, Orford-avenue, has won the D.C.M., for valyble services rendered during a bombing expedition -a few weeks ago. It was in a letter dated May 11th that Private Heesom first mentioned the bombing exploit, when he wrote :—" I am still having good luck, , but I shall never forget the other night. I went oht with a party and raided the German ' trenches, and we did give it them. I. shall never forget when we were coming back. again, either. But don't , worry, I am quite all right. We got commended by the .Divisional Com- mander and also by the Brigadier-General. The Colonel asked me how old I was, and when I

KILLED. PRIVATE J. BRIIVIELOW. News Of Death Aftw Eleven Months' Waiting. After waiting anxiously for eleven months an official intimation has this week reached Mr. and Mrs. Richard Brimelow, 22, Pickmeie- street, to the effect that their son, Private John Brimelow, was killed in action on June 16th, 1915. Private Brimelow was reported missing after taking, part in a charge with the War- rington Territorials in June, and since then nothing further had been heard. Private Brimelow, who was •22 years of age,

WOUNDED. RIFLEMAN S. DICKENSON. Loses Left Eye And Is Injured Elsewhere. Mrs. Dickenson, a widow, residing at 41, ForSter-street, has been informed that her son, Rifleman Stanley Dickenson, of the Rifle Bri-- gade, has been seriously wounded. A notifica- tion from the War Office was first received, to be closely followed by the following letter, written by Rifleman Dickenson himself : `` I suppose you.will have heard by now about my gettind wounded. MR. A. ,H. POLLEN. From Grand Fleet To Royal Court Theatre Next Week. Mr. A. H. Pollen, the great naval expert, was to have been at the Royal Court Theatre to-day, but an invitation to view the Grand Fleet im- pelled him to cancel the engagement, and he is now having a wonderful experience at sea. He will return towards the end of the week, and on Saturday, June 3rd, come to the Royal Court and tell us all about it. We are, indeed, for- ; tunate. We shall get our Navy news quite hot [ and straight from the horse's mouth, as a popu- lar phrase has it. All England knows Mr. Pollen as a writer and lecturer. In his first ,capacity we are continu- ally judging of his merits and awarding him the palm for his knowledge and lucidity. As a lecturer, he is equally successful, and his plat- form expositions have frequently attracted Mr. Balfour, in fact, that gentleman recently took the chair at one of his lectures and paid him a high tribute. The Difference. A Dublin office boy, returning to his employ- ment after a fortnight's absence, informed his employer that he had been fighting and a prisoner; whereas, of course, in similar circum- stances, an English lad would have contented himself with explaining that he had merely been taking the letters to the post.—" Punch." The Last Straw. Arthur: "They say, dear, that people who live together get to look alike." Kate: "Then you must consider my refusal as final."
